At any rate, I realized about half past nine that Parnassus was on a much rougher road than the highway had any right to be, and there were no telephone poles to be seen. I knew that they stretched all along the main road, so plainly I had made a mistake. I was reluctant for a moment to admit that I could be wrong, and just then Peg stumbled heavily and stood still. She paid no heed to my exhortations, and when I got out and carried my lantern to see whether anything was in the way, I found that she had cast a shoe and her foot was bleeding. The shoe must have dropped off some way back and she had picked up a nail or something in the quick. I saw no alternative but to stay where I was for the night.

===Chinese===
显然，教授是这一家子世代相传的英雄，我开始觉得自己活像个冒牌货！

我知道这有些犯傻，可我已打定主意，当夜要赶去伍德布里奇。路远不过 4 英里，时间也才 8 点刚过。我心里掠过一丝全然不值当的懊恼，因为自己仍走在教授威望的光环底下。普拉特一家张口闭口离不了他，我想去一个按我本身的价值来掂量我的地方，而不是只当他的门徒。“这红胡子，真该死，”我暗自说，“我看他是把这些人迷住心了！”我不顾他们的一再挽留和留宿的邀请，执意套上了佩格。我送给他们那本《丛林之书》，算是对他们款待的一点回报，末了又卖给普拉特先生一册兰姆《莎士比亚戏剧故事集》的小字本，我想这本他读起来不至于犯脑热病。随后我点亮灯笼，在一阵道别声中，帕纳索斯号隆隆上路了。“唉，”重新拐上大路时我暗自嘀咕，“这小姜饼真见鬼，他好像能把人人都催眠了……这会儿他该快到布鲁克林了吧！”

路上极静，也极黑，天阴了，月亮星星一概看不见。本是条直路，照理不该出错，想必是我打了个盹，佩格趁势拐错了弯。总之，9 点半光景我发觉帕纳索斯号走在一条比任何像样的公路都颠簸得多的路上，而且看不见电线杆。我知道大路沿线电线杆一根连一根，明摆着是我走岔了。我一时还不情愿认错，恰在这时，佩格重重一个趔趄，站住不动了。它不理会我的吆喝，我提着灯笼下车查看挡路的是什么，才发现它掉了一只掌铁，蹄子正在流血。掌铁想必掉在后面什么地方，它又在蹄肉里扎进了一根钉子或别的什么。我看别无他法，只好原地过夜。

===Original===
This was not very pleasant, but the adventures of the day had put me into a stoical frame of mind, and I saw no good in repining. I unhitched Peg, sponged her foot, and tied her to a tree. I would have made more careful explorations to determine just where I was, but a sharp patter of rain began to fall. So I climbed into my Parnassus, took Bock in with me, and lit the swinging lamp. By this time it was nearly ten o’clock. There was nothing to do but turn in, so I took off my boots and lay down in the bunk. Bock lay quite comfortably on the floor of the van. I meant to read for a while, and so did not turn out the light, but I fell asleep almost immediately.

I woke up at half past eleven and turned out the lamp, which had made the van very warm. I opened the little windows front and back, and would have opened the door, but I feared Bock might slip away. It was still raining a little. To my annoyance I felt very wakeful. I lay for some time listening to the patter of raindrops on the roof and skylight﻿—a very snug sound when one is warm and safe. Every now and then I could hear Peg stamping in the underbrush. I was almost dozing off again when Bock gave a low growl.

No woman of my bulk has a right to be nervous, I guess, but instantly my security vanished! The patter of the rain seemed menacing, and I imagined a hundred horrors. I was totally alone and unarmed, and Bock was not a large dog. He growled again, and I felt worse than before. I imagined that I heard stealthy sounds in the bushes, and once Peg snorted as though frightened. I put my hand down to pat Bock, and found that his neck was all bristly, like a fighting cock. He uttered a queer half growl, half whine, which gave me a chill. Someone must be prowling about the van, but in the falling rain I could hear nothing.

===Chinese===
这可不怎么惬意，但这一天的种种奇遇已把我磨出了一副听天由命的心境，抱怨无益。我卸下佩格，用海绵擦净它的蹄子，把它拴在一棵树上。本想再仔细探探自己究竟身在何处，可雨点已经噼噼啪啪落了下来。于是我爬进我的帕纳索斯号，把博克也带进来，点亮了吊灯。这时已近 10 点。除了安寝无事可做，我便脱掉靴子，在铺位上躺下。博克舒舒服服卧在车厢地板上。我本想看一会儿书，所以没熄灯，却几乎立刻睡着了。

11 点半我醒了，吹灭了油灯——灯把车厢烘得很热。我打开前后的小窗，本想连门也开了，又怕博克溜出去。雨还在零星地下。恼人的是，我睡意全消。我躺了好一阵，听着雨点敲在顶棚和天窗上的啪啪声——人又暖和又安稳时，这声音听着真受用。不时能听见佩格在灌木丛里刨蹄。我眼看又要迷糊过去，博克忽然低吼了一声。

我这般块头的女人，照理没资格神经紧张，可我的安全感霎时无影无踪！雨点声听上去凶险四伏，我在想象中演过了一百种恐怖。我孤身一人，手无寸铁，博克又不是条大狗。它又吼了一声，我比方才更慌了。我仿佛听见灌木丛里鬼鬼祟祟的响动，佩格有一回也喷着响鼻，像是受了惊。我伸手去抚博克，发现它颈毛根根倒竖，像只斗鸡。它喉咙里发出半是低吼、半是呜咽的怪声，叫我脊背发凉。一定有人在车厢四周打转，可雨声之中我什么也听不见。

===Original===
I felt I must do something. I was afraid to call out lest I betray the fact that there was only a woman in the van. My expedient was absurd enough, but at any rate it satisfied my desire to act. I seized one of my boots and banged vigorously on the floor, at the same time growling in as deep and masculine a voice as I could muster: “What the hell’s the matter? What the hell’s the matter?” This sounds silly enough, I dare say, but it afforded me some relief. And as Bock shortly ceased growling, it apparently served some purpose.

I lay awake for a long time, tingling all over with nervousness. Then I began to grow calmer, and was getting drowsy almost in spite of myself when I was aroused by the unmistakable sound of Bock’s tail thumping on the floor﻿—a sure sign of pleasure. This puzzled me quite as much as his growls. I did not dare strike a light, but could hear him sniffing at the door of the van and whining with eagerness. This seemed very uncanny, and again I crept stealthily out of the bunk and pounded on the floor lustily, this time with the frying pan, which made an unearthly din. Peg neighed and snorted, and Bock began to bark. Even in my anxiety I almost laughed. “It sounds like an insane asylum,” I thought, and reflected that probably the disturbance was only caused by some small animal. Perhaps a rabbit or a skunk which Bock had winded and wanted to chase. I patted him, and crawled into my bunk once more.

But my real excitement was still to come. About half an hour later I heard unmistakable footsteps alongside the van. Bock growled furiously, and I lay in a panic. Something jarred one of the wheels. Then broke out a most extraordinary racket. I heard quick steps, Peg whinneyed, and something fell heavily against the back of the wagon. There was a violent scuffle on the ground, the sound of blows, and rapid breathing. With my heart jumping I peered out of one of the back windows. There was barely any light, but dimly I could see a tumbling mass which squirmed and writhed on the ground. Something struck one of the rear wheels so that Parnassus trembled. I heard hoarse swearing, and then the whole body, whatever it was, rolled off into the underbrush. There was a terrific crashing and snapping of twigs. Bock whined, growled, and pawed madly at the door. And then complete silence.

===Chinese===
我觉得非做点什么不可。我不敢喊叫，生怕暴露车里只有一个女人。我的应急招数够荒唐的，但好歹满足了我要行动的渴望。我抓起一只靴子，在地板上咚咚猛敲，同时用我能憋出的最低沉、最男人腔的嗓音吼道：“他妈的怎么回事？他妈的怎么回事？”这听上去是够蠢的，我承认，可它多少让我宽了宽心。而且博克不久就停止了低吼，可见多少起了点作用。

我久久醒着躺，浑身的神经刺刺地发麻。后来渐渐平静下来，正不由自主地蒙眬欲睡，忽然被一阵错不了的声音惊起——博克的尾巴在地板上啪嗒啪嗒直拍，那是欢喜的可靠记号。这和它的低吼一样叫我纳闷。我不敢点火，却听得见它在车厢门口嗅个不停，急得呜呜直叫。这情形邪门得很，我又蹑手蹑脚爬出铺位，这回抡起长柄煎锅狠狠敲地板，敲出一片鬼哭狼嚎的动静。佩格嘶鸣喷鼻，博克开腔狂吠。纵然满心焦灼，我差点笑出声。“听着跟疯人院似的，”我想，转念琢磨，这番骚动多半不过是只什么小兽闹的。兴许是只兔子或臭鼬，被博克闻见了味儿，想追出去。我拍了拍它，重新爬进铺位。

可真正让我魂飞魄散的还在后头。约莫半个钟头后，我听见车厢旁边传来确凿无疑的脚步声。博克狂怒地低吼，我吓得魂不附体地躺着。什么东西撞了一下车轮。接着爆发出一场天大的喧闹。急促的脚步，佩格的嘶鸣，有什么东西重重砸在车后板上。地面上是一场凶猛的扭打，拳脚声、急促的喘气声乱作一团。我心口狂跳，从一扇后窗向外窥探。几乎没有光，只依稀看见一团翻滚的东西在地上蠕动挣扎。什么东西撞上后轮，撞得帕纳索斯号直发抖。我听见嘶哑的咒骂，随后那整个一团——不管它是什么——滚进了灌木丛。枝条劈啪乱响，一阵骇人的碎裂声。博克呜咽着、低吼着，疯了似的扒门。然后，一片死寂。

===Original===
My nerves were quite shattered by this time. I don’t think I had been so frightened since childhood days when I awakened from a nightmare. Little trickles of fear crept up and down my spine and my scalp prickled. I pulled Bock on the bunk, and lay with one hand on his collar. He, too, seemed agitated and sniffed gingerly now and then. Finally, however, he gave a sigh and fell asleep. I judged it might have been two o’clock, but I did not like to strike a light. And at last I fell into a doze.

When I woke the sun was shining brilliantly and the air was full of the chirping of birds. I felt stiff and uneasy from sleeping in my clothes, and my foot was numb from Bock’s weight.

I got up and looked out of the window. Parnassus was standing in a narrow lane by a grove of birch trees. The ground was muddy, and smeared with footprints behind the van. I opened the door and looked around. The first thing I saw, on the ground by one of the wheels, was a battered tweed cap.

===Chinese===
这时我的神经彻底散了架。打小时候做噩梦惊醒以来，我还没那么害怕过。细细的恐惧顺着脊椎爬上爬下，头皮根根发麻。我把博克拽上铺位，一只手搭在它的项圈上躺着。它似乎也心神不宁，时不时小心翼翼地嗅着。不过末了，它叹了口气，睡着了。我估摸该有两点了，可我不愿点灯。终于，我也迷糊了过去。

醒来时阳光明晃晃地照着，空气里满是鸟雀的啁啾。和衣而睡，浑身又僵又难受，脚也被博克压得发麻。

我起身朝窗外望去。帕纳索斯号停在一条窄巷里，旁边是一片白桦林。地上一片泥泞，车后拖着一串乱糟糟的脚印。我打开门四下张望。头一样入眼的东西，是一只车轮旁地上的、揉得稀烂的粗花呢帽子。

## X / 十

===Original===
We stood in complete dismay﻿—I did, at any rate﻿—for about as long as it takes to peel a potato. There could be no doubt in which direction the van had moved, for the track of the wheels was plain. It had gone farther up the lane toward the quarry. In the earth, which was still soggy, were a number of footprints.

“By the bones of Polycarp!” exclaimed the Professor, “those hoboes have stolen the van. I guess they think it’ll make a fine Pullman sleeper for them. If I’d realized there was more than one of them I’d have hung around closer. They need a lesson.”

Good Lord! I thought, here’s Don Quixote about to wade into another fight.

===Chinese===
我们惊愕失措地站在原地——至少我是这样——站了大约削一只土豆的工夫。车往哪个方向去了，毫无疑义，因为轮辙清清楚楚。它顺着巷子朝采石场方向去了。还湿漉漉的泥地上，留着好些脚印。

“凭波利卡普（Polycarp）的骸骨起誓！”教授失声道，“那些流浪汉把车偷走了。我猜他们是想拿它当一节顶呱呱的普尔曼卧铺。早知道他们不止一个人，我就跟得再近些了。得给他们上一课。”

天哪！我心想，这位堂吉诃德又要杀进另一场混战里去了。

===Original===
“Hadn’t we better go back and get Mr. Pratt?” I asked.

This was obviously the wrong thing to say. It put the fiery little man all the more on his mettle. His beard bristled. “Nothing of the sort!” he said. “Those fellows are cowards and vagabonds anyway. They can’t be far off; you haven’t been away more than an hour, have you? If they’ve done anything to Bock, by the bones of Chaucer, I’ll harry them. I *thought* I heard him bark.”

He hurried up the lane, and I followed in a panicky frame of mind. The track wound along a hillside, between a high bank and a forest of birch trees. I think the distance can’t have been more than a quarter of a mile. Anyway, in a very few minutes the road made a sharp twist to the right and we found ourselves looking down into the quarry, over a sheer rocky drop of a hundred feet at least. Below, drawn over to one side of the wall of rock, stood Parnassus. Peg was between the shafts. Bock was nowhere to be seen. Sitting by the van were three disreputable looking men. The smoke of a cooking fire rose into the air; evidently they were making free with my little larder.

===Chinese===
“咱们是不是回去搬普拉特先生？”我问。

这话说得显然不是时候。它把这名火性子的小个子男人的犟劲儿激得更足了。他的胡子根根乍起。“没那个道理！”他说，“那帮家伙本来就是懦夫加无赖。他们跑不远；你走了总不见得超过一个钟头吧？他们要是动了博克一根毛，凭乔叟（Chaucer）的骸骨起誓，我跟他们没完。我方才真听见它叫了。”

他快步冲上巷子，我心慌意乱地跟上。小径沿着山坡蜿蜒，一边是高高的土坎，一边是一片白桦林。我想那距离不过四分之一英里。反正没过几分钟，路猛地向右一拐，我们已站在采石场边上，朝下俯瞰——一道笔直的岩壁，深至少 100 英尺。底下，帕纳索斯号停在岩壁一侧。佩格套在车辕里。博克却不见了踪影。车边坐着三个面目不大体面的男人。一堆野炊的烟升上半空；显然，他们正随意享用我的小食柜。

===Original===
“Keep back,” said the Professor softly. “Don’t let them see us.” He flattened himself in the grass and crawled to the edge of the cliff. I did the same, and we lay there, invisible from below, but quite able to see everything in the quarry. The three tramps were evidently enjoying an excellent breakfast.

“This place is a regular hangout for these fellows,” Mifflin whispered. “I’ve seen hoboes about here every year. They go into winter quarters about the end of October, usually. There’s an old blasted out section of this quarry that makes a sheltered dormitory for them, and as the place isn’t worked any more they’re not disturbed here so long as they don’t make mischief in the neighbourhood. We’ll give them﻿ ﻿…”

“Hands up!” said a rough voice behind us. I looked round. There was a fat, red faced villainous looking creature covering us with a shiny revolver. It was an awkward situation. Both the Professor and I were lying full length on the ground. We were quite helpless.

===Chinese===
“别过去，”教授低声说，“别让他们瞧见咱们。”他把身子贴伏在草里，爬到崖边。我照着他的样子做。我们趴在那儿，底下看不见，采石场里的一切却尽收眼底。那三个流浪汉显然正美滋滋地享用一顿丰盛的早餐。

“这地方是这帮家伙的老窝，”米夫林悄声说，“我年年都在这一带见着流浪汉。他们通常 10 月底就进窝过冬。采石场有一段炸出来的旧石膛，正好给他们当避风的宿舍；这地方反正不再开采，只要他们不在左近惹事，也没人来搅扰他们。咱们得给他们……”

“举起手来！”背后响起一个粗哑的嗓门。我回头看去。一个肥头大耳、红脸膛、一脸歹相的家伙，正用一把亮闪闪的左轮枪指着我们。局面尴尬得很。教授和我都直挺挺趴在地上，全然无从招架。

===Original===
“Get up!” said the tramp in a husky, nasty voice. “I guess youse thought we wasn’t covering our trail? Well, we’ll have to tie you up, I reckon, while we get away with this Crystal Pallis of yourn.”

I scrambled to my feet, but to my surprise the Professor continued to lie at full length.

“Get up, deacon!” said the tramp again. “Get up on them graceful limbs, *if* you please.”

I guess he thought himself safe from attack by a woman. At any rate, he bent over as if to grab Mifflin by the neck. I saw my chance and jumped on him from behind. I am heavy, as I have said, and he sprawled on the ground. My doubts as to the pistol being loaded were promptly dissolved, for it went off like a cannon. Nobody was in front of it, however, and Mifflin was on his feet like a flash. He had the ruffian by the throat and kicked the weapon out of his hand. I ran to seize it.

===Chinese===
“起来！”流浪汉用沙哑凶狠的嗓音说，“你们八成当我们没留后手吧？得，看来得把你们捆上，趁咱们开走你们这座‘水晶大宫’。”

我手忙脚乱地爬起来，可出乎意料，教授照旧直挺挺躺着不动。

“起来，牧师老爷！”流浪汉又喝道，“劳驾，抬起您那两根体面的腿。”

我估摸他是觉得，女人家不会动手。总之，他弯下腰，像要去揪米夫林的脖子。我瞅准时机，从背后朝他扑了上去。我说过，我有分量，他扑通一声趴在了地上。我原本还疑心那枪里没子弹，这疑心顿时烟消云散——枪响得跟炮似的。好在枪口前头没人，米夫林也一闪身蹿了起来。他掐住那恶棍的喉咙，一脚把枪踢离他的手。我跑过去把枪捡住。

===Original===
“You son of Satan!” said the valiant Redbeard. “Thought you could bully us, did you? Miss McGill, you were as quick as Joan of Arc. Hand me the pistol, please.”

I gave it to him, and he shoved it under the hobo’s nose.

“Now,” he said, “take off that rag around your neck.”

The rag was an old red handkerchief, inconceivably soiled. The tramp removed it, grumbling and whining. Mifflin gave me the pistol to hold while he tied our prisoner’s wrists together. In the meantime we heard a shout from the quarry. The three vagabonds were gazing up in great excitement.

===Chinese===
“你这撒旦的崽子！”英勇的红胡子喝道，“以为能欺负我们，嗯？麦吉尔小姐，你快得像圣女贞德。请把枪递给我。”

我把枪给他，他把枪口顶到流浪汉的鼻子底下。

“现在，”他说，“把脖子上那块破布解下来。”

那破布是一条旧红手帕，脏得没法形容。流浪汉一边嘟囔一边哀叫，把它解了下来。米夫林把枪交给我端着，自己捆我们俘虏的手腕。就在这时，采石场里传来一声喊。那三个无赖正激动地朝上张望。

===Original===
“You tell those fashion plates down there,” said Mifflin, as he knotted the tramp’s hands together, “that if they make any fight I’ll shoot them like crows.” His voice was cold and savage and he seemed quite master of the situation, but I must confess I wondered how we could handle four of them.

The greasy ruffian shouted down to his pals in the quarry, but I did not hear what he said, as just then the Professor asked me to keep our captive covered while he got a stick. I stood with the pistol pointed at his head while Mifflin ran back into the birchwood to cut a cudgel.

The tramp’s face became the colour of the under side of a fried egg as he looked into the muzzle of his own gun.

“Say, lady,” he pleaded, “that gun goes off awful easy, point her somewhere else or you’ll croak me by mistake.”

===Chinese===
“你告诉底下那几位时装画报上的爷们，”米夫林一边打结捆牢流浪汉的双手，一边说，“他们要是敢动手，我就把他们当乌鸦打。”他的嗓音冷而狠，似乎全然掌控着局面；可我必须承认，我心里直犯嘀咕：他们一共四个，我们怎么对付得了。

那油光满面的恶棍朝采石场里的同伙喊话，可我没听清他说了什么，因为教授这时正叫我拿枪看住俘虏，他自己去找根棍子。我用枪指着他脑袋站定，米夫林跑回白桦林里去砍一根短棒。

流浪汉望着自己那杆枪的枪口，脸变成了煎蛋底面的颜色。

“哎，太太，”他告饶道，“这枪可灵得很，一碰就走火。您把枪口挪挪地方，别一个失手把我给崩了。”

===Original===
I thought a good scare wouldn’t do him any harm and kept the barrel steadily on him.

The rascals down below seemed debating what to do. I don’t know whether they were armed or not; but probably they imagined that there were more than two of us. At all events, by the time Mifflin came back with a stout birch staff they were hustling out of the quarry on the lower side. The Professor swore, and looked as if he would gladly give chase, but he refrained.

“Here, you,” he said in crisp tones to the tramp, “march on ahead of us, down to the quarry.”

===Chinese===
我想，好好吓他一吓不无好处，便让枪口稳稳对着他。

底下那几个坏蛋似乎在争执怎么办。我不知道他们带没带家伙；多半是他们猜想我们不止两个人。总之，等米夫林拎着一根结实的白桦棒回来，他们已经从采石场下口一哄而散了。教授骂了一句，一副恨不得追下去的神气，到底还是忍住了。

“喂，你，”他对流浪汉利落地说，“走前头，带我们下采石场去。”

===Original===
The fat ruffian shambled awkwardly down the trail. We had to make quite a detour to get into the quarry, and by the time we reached there the other three tramps had got clean away. I was not sorry, to tell the truth. I thought the Professor had had enough scrapping for one twenty four hours.

Peg whinneyed loudly as she saw us coming, but Bock was not in sight.

“What have you done with the dog, you swine?” said Mifflin. “If you’ve hurt him I’ll make you pay with your own hide.”

===Chinese===
胖子恶棍深一脚浅一脚地挪下小径。进采石场得绕好大一圈，等我们赶到，另外三个流浪汉早跑得没影了。说实话，我并不遗憾。依我看， 24 个小时里打的架，教授已经够本了。

佩格望见我们来，高声响了个鼻，可还是不见博克的影子。

“你们把狗怎么了，你们这群猪猡？”米夫林说，“它要是伤了一根毛，我拿你这张皮抵账。”

===Original===
Our prisoner was completely cowed. “No, boss, we ain’t hurt the dog,” he fawned. “We tied him up so he couldn’t bark, that’s all. He’s in the bus.” And sure enough, by this time we could hear smothered yelping and whining from Parnassus.

I hurried to open the door, and there was Bock, his jaws tied together with a rope-end. He bounded out and made super-canine efforts to express his joy at seeing the Professor again. He paid very little attention to me.

“Well,” said Mifflin, after freeing the dog’s muzzle, and with difficulty restraining him from burying his teeth in the tramp’s shin, “what shall we do with this heroic specimen of manhood? Shall we cart him over to the jail in Port Vigor, or shall we let him go?”

===Chinese===
我们的俘虏彻底蔫了。“没伤着，头儿，我们没动那狗，”他讨好地说，“就是把它捆上，免得它叫唤，就这点事。它在车里呢。”果不其然，这时我们已听得见帕纳索斯号里闷闷的吠声和呜咽。

我赶紧打开车门，博克果然在里头，嘴巴被一截绳头捆着。它蹦出来，使出超越犬类的浑身解数，表达再见到教授的欢喜。我嘛，它几乎没理会。

“唉，”米夫林解开狗嘴上的绳，又好不容易拦住它不去咬流浪汉的胫骨，说，“这条男子汉里的英雄样板，咱们怎么处置？把他押到波特维戈的监狱去，还是放他走？”

===Original===
The tramp burst into a whining appeal that was almost funny, it was so abject. The Professor cut it short.

“I ought to pack you into quod,” he said. “Are you the Phoebus Apollo I scuffled with down the lane last night? Was it you skulking around this wagon then?”

“No, boss, that was Splitlip Sam, honest to Gawd it was. He come back, boss; said he’d been fightin’ with a cat-o’-mountain! Say, boss, you sure hit him hard. One of his lamps is a pudding! Boss, I’ll swear I ain’t had nothin’ to do with it.”

“I don’t like your society,” said the Professor, “and I’m going to turn you loose. I’m going to count ten, and if you’re not out of this quarry by then, I’ll shoot. And if I see you again I’ll skin you alive. Now get out!”

===Chinese===
流浪汉哇地一下哭诉起来，那副卑躬屈膝的可怜相，简直叫人好笑。教授打断了他。

“按理该把你塞进班房，”他说，“昨晚在巷子里跟我扭打的那位福玻斯·阿波罗（Phoebus Apollo），就是你吗？当时围着这辆车鬼鬼祟祟转悠的，也是你？”

“不是，头儿，那是豁嘴山姆（Splitlip Sam），老天爷在上，真是他。他回去啦，头儿，说他跟一头山猫干了一仗！嗐，头儿，您下手可真够狠的。他一只‘灯盏’肿成了烂桃！头儿，我起誓，这事我一星半点儿也没沾。”

“我不喜欢跟你打交道，”教授说，“打算放你走。我数到十，你要是还没滚出这采石场，我就开枪。往后要是再让我撞见，我活剥了你的皮。滚吧！”

===Original===
He cut the knotted handkerchief in two. The hobo needed no urging. He spun on his heel and fled like a rabbit. The Professor watched him go, and as the fat, ungainly figure burst through a hedge and disappeared he fired the revolver into the air to frighten him still more. Then he tossed the weapon into the pool near by.

“Well, Miss McGill,” he said with a chuckle, “if you like to undertake breakfast, I’ll fix up Peg.” And he drew the horseshoe from his pocket once more.

A brief inspection of Parnassus satisfied me that the thieves had not had time to do any real damage. They had got out most of the eatables and spread them on a flat rock in preparation for a feast; and they had tracked a good deal of mud into the van; but otherwise I could see nothing amiss. So while Mifflin busied himself with Peg’s foot it was easy for me to get a meal under way. I found a gush of clean water trickling down the face of the rock. There were still some eggs and bread and cheese in the little cupboard, and an unopened tin of condensed milk. I gave Peg her nose bag of oats, and fed Bock, who was frisking about in high spirits. By that time the shoeing was done, and the Professor and I sat down to an improvised meal. I was beginning to feel as if this gypsy existence were the normal course of my life.

===Chinese===
他把打结的手帕割成两段。流浪汉不用人催，脚跟一转，兔子似的窜了。教授望着他跑远，等那肥硕笨拙的身影撞开一道树篱、消失不见，又朝天空放了一枪，好吓他个魂飞魄散。然后把枪扔进了近旁的水潭里。

“好了，麦吉尔小姐，”他呵呵一笑说，“你要是愿意操办早饭，我就去收拾佩格的蹄子。”说着又从口袋里掏出了那块马掌铁。

我把帕纳索斯号粗粗查验了一遍，放了心：贼还来不及造成真正的损坏。他们把大多数吃食搬了出来，摊在一块平石上，正打算大吃一顿；车里也叫他们踩进好多泥；除此之外我看不出什么毛病。于是米夫林忙佩格的蹄子时，我不费事就把一顿饭张罗起来了。岩壁上有一道清洁的泉水淌下来。小橱柜里还剩些鸡蛋、面包和奶酪，还有一罐没开封的炼乳。我给佩格挂上装燕麦的料袋，又喂了博克，它正撒着欢儿地蹦跶。这时掌铁也钉好了，教授和我坐下吃这顿因陋就简的饭。我开始觉得，这种吉卜赛日子仿佛就是我人生的正轨了。

===Original===
“Well, Professor,” I said, as I handed him a cup of coffee and a plate of scrambled eggs and cheese, “for a man who slept in a wet haystack, you acquit yourself with excellent valour.”

“Old Parnassus is quite a stormy petrel,” he said. “I used to think the chief difficulty in writing a book would be to invent things to happen, but if I were to sit down and write the adventures I’d had with her it would be a regular Odyssey.”

“How about Peg’s foot?” I asked. “Can she travel on it?”

“It’ll be all right if you go easy. I’ve scraped out the injured part and put the shoe back. I keep a little kit of tools under the van for emergencies of all sorts.”

===Chinese===
“唉，教授，”我递给他一杯咖啡、一盘奶酪炒蛋，说，“睡了一宿湿草堆的人，打起交道来倒是骁勇得很。”

“老帕纳索斯号真是只风暴海燕，”他说，“我从前总以为，写书最大的难处是编排出事情来让它们发生；可我要是坐下来把她这一路的奇遇写出来，那就是一部地道的《奥德赛》。”

“佩格的蹄子怎么样？”我问。“还能走吗？”

“慢慢走就没事。我把受伤的地方刮净了，掌铁也钉回去了。车厢底下我备着一小箱工具，各样急用都使得上。”

===Original===
It was chilly, and we didn’t dawdle over our meal. I only made a feint of eating, as I had had a little breakfast before, and also as the events of the last few hours had left me rather restless. I wanted to get Parnassus out on the highway again, to jog along in the sun and think things over. The quarry was a desolate, forbidding place anyway. But before we left we explored the cave where the tramps had been preparing to make themselves comfortable for the winter. It was not really a cave, but only a shaft into the granite cliff. A screen of evergreen boughs protected the opening against the weather, and inside were piles of sacking that had evidently been used as beds, and many old grocery boxes for tables and chairs. It amused me to notice a cracked fragment of mirror balanced on a corner of rock. Even these ragamuffins apparently were not totally unconscious of personal appearance. I seized the opportunity, while the Professor was giving Peg’s foot a final look, to rearrange my hair, which was emphatically a sight. I hardly think Andrew would have recognized me that morning.

We led Peg up the steep incline, back into the lane where I had strayed, and at length we reached the main road again. Here I began to lay down the law to Redbeard.

===Chinese===
天气凉，我们吃饭也没磨蹭。我只是比划着吃了点，一来我先前进过一点早餐，二来这几个钟头的事情闹得我心里安定不下来。我想赶紧把帕纳索斯号赶回大路上，在太阳底下慢慢悠悠地走，把事情细细想清楚。那采石场本来就是个荒凉瘆人的地方。不过临走之前，我们探了探流浪汉们准备过冬的洞穴。其实算不得洞，只是花岗岩崖壁里的一道石膛。洞口挂着一面常绿树枝编的帘子挡风遮雨；里头是一堆堆麻袋，显然当床铺用，还有不少旧杂货箱充作桌椅。有个细节叫我好笑：一块裂了纹的镜子碎片，架在一方岩角上。看来连这些破衣烂衫的汉子，也不至于全然不顾自己的仪表。趁教授最后检查佩格蹄子的当口，我抓住机会重新拢了拢头发——那实在是没法见人的样子。我看那天早上，安德鲁多半认不出我来。

我们牵着佩格爬上陡坡，回到我走岔的那条巷子，终于又上了大路。到了这儿，我便对红胡子发号施令起来。

===Original===
“Now look here, Professor,” I said, “I’m not going to have you tramp all the way back to Port Vigor. After the night you’ve had you need a rest. You just climb into that Parnassus and lie down for a good snooze. I’ll drive you into Woodbridge and you can take your train there. Now you get right into that bunk. I’ll sit out here and drive.”

He demurred, but without much emphasis. I think the little fool was just about fagged out, and no wonder. I was a trifle groggy myself. In the end he was quite docile. He climbed into the van, took off his boots, and lay down under a blanket. Bock followed him, and I think they both fell asleep on the instant. I got on the front seat and took the reins. I didn’t let Peg go more quickly than a walk as I wanted to spare her sore foot.

My, what a morning that was after the rain! The road ran pretty close to the shore, and every now and then I could catch a glimpse of the water. The air was keen﻿—not just the ordinary, unnoticed air that we breathe in and out and don’t think about, but a sharp and tingling essence, as strong in the nostrils as camphor or ammonia. The sun seemed focused upon Parnassus, and we moved along the white road in a flush of golden light. The flat fronds of the cedars swayed gently in the salty air, and for the first time in ten years, I should think, I began amusing myself by selecting words to describe the goodness of the morning. I even imagined myself writing a description of it, as if I were Andrew or Thoreau. The crazy little Professor had inoculated me with his literary bug, I guess.

===Chinese===
“你听好，教授，”我说，“我不能让你一路步行回波特维戈去。你熬了那样一夜，需要歇歇。你就爬进帕纳索斯号，躺下好好睡一觉。我把你捎到伍德布里奇，你在那儿上火车。这就给我躺到铺位上去。我坐外头赶车。”

他推辞了一下，可没怎么坚持。我想这小傻瓜已经累得快散架了，也难怪。我自己都有点头晕腿软。末了他乖乖听话了。他爬进车厢，脱了靴子，和衣躺在毯子底下。博克跟着他进去，我看他们俩是转眼就睡着了。我登上前座，接过缰绳。佩格的蹄子还疼着，我不叫它走得比散步快。

哎呀，雨后的那个早晨啊！大路离海岸很近，时不时能望见一眼海水。空气凛冽清新——不是我们平常吸进呼出、想也不想的那种空气，而是一种扎人又提神的精华，冲着鼻孔，跟樟脑、氨水一样有劲。太阳像是把光都聚在帕纳索斯号上，我们沐着一派金色的光晕，沿着白色的大路前行。雪松扁平的枝叶在带咸味的空气里轻轻摇曳。我想，这是 10 年来头一回，我开始自得其乐地挑拣字眼，来描画这个早晨的美好。我甚至想象自己在写一段描写，仿佛我是安德鲁，或者梭罗（Thoreau）。我猜，这个疯疯癫癫的小教授，把他的文学病菌种进了我身上。

===Original===
And then I did a dishonourable thing. Just by chance I put my hand into the little pocket beside the seat where Mifflin kept a few odds and ends. I meant to have another look at that card of his with the poem on it. And there I found a funny, battered little notebook, evidently forgotten. On the cover was written, in ink, “Thoughts on the Present Discontents.” That title seemed vaguely familiar. I seemed to recall something of the kind from my school days﻿—more than twenty years ago, goodness me! Of course if I had been honourable I wouldn’t have looked into it. But in a kind of quibbling self-justification I recalled that I had bought Parnassus and all it contained, “lock, stock, barrel and bung” as Andrew used to say. And so﻿ ﻿…

The notebook was full of little jottings, written in pencil in the Professor’s small, precise hand. The words were rubbed and soiled, but plainly legible. I read this:

===Chinese===
接着，我干了一件不光彩的事。我随手伸进座位旁边那个小口袋——米夫林在里头搁些零七碎八的小东西。我本想再看看他那张写着诗的卡片。却在里头摸到一个皱皱巴巴的小笔记本，显然是忘下的。封面用墨水写着一行字：《关于当下不满的随想》。这题目隐隐有点眼熟。我似乎想起学生时代读过类似的东西——老天爷，那都是 20 多年前的事了！当然，我要是正派，就不会翻开它。可我心虚地自我开脱，想起我买下的帕纳索斯号和它装的一切——用安德鲁的话说，“枪机、枪托、枪管连桶塞”一并买下的。于是……

笔记本里满是小小的札记，用铅笔写成，是教授那手细小而工整的字。字迹磨得发乌、沾着污渍，却清晰可辨。我读到这样几段：

===Original===
> I don’t suppose Bock or Peg get lonely, but by the bones of Ben Gunn, I do. Seems silly when Herrick and Hans Andersen and Tennyson and Thoreau and a whole wagonload of other good fellows are riding at my back. I can hear them all talking as we trundle along. But books aren’t a *substantial* world after all, and every now and then we get hungry for some closer, more human relationships. I’ve been totally alone now for eight years﻿—except for Runt, and he might be dead and never say so. This wandering about is fine in its way, but it must come to an end some day. A man needs to put down a root somewhere to be really happy.

> What absurd victims of contrary desires we are! If a man is settled in one place he yearns to wander; when he wanders he yearns to have a home. And yet how bestial is content﻿—all the great things in life are done by discontented people.

> There are three ingredients in the good life: learning, earning, and yearning. A man should be learning as he goes; and he should be earning bread for himself and others; and he should be yearning, too: yearning to know the unknowable.

> What a fine old poem is “The Pulley” by George Herbert! Those Elizabethan fellows knew how to write! They were marred perhaps by their idea that poems must be “witty.” (Remember how Bacon said that reading poets makes one witty? There he gave a clue to the literature of his time.) Their fantastic puns and conceits are rather out of our fashion nowadays. But Lord! the root of the matter was in them! How gallantly, how reverently, they tackle the problems of life!

===Chinese===
> 我想博克和佩格是不懂得寂寞的，可凭本·冈恩（Ben Gunn）的骸骨起誓，我懂。说来可笑，赫里克、安徒生、丁尼生、梭罗，还有满满一车的别的良朋益友，都坐在我的背后。车轱辘转动时，我听得见他们全都在说话。可书籍到底不是一个“结结实实”的世界，隔三差五，我们就饥渴起来，想要更贴近、更有人味儿的往来。如今我孤身一人已经 8 年了——除了朗特（Runt），而他说不定早死了都不吭一声。这样四处流浪自有它的好处，可总有一天得有个尽头。人总得在什么地方扎下根，才算真正快活。

> 我们这些生灵，做了相互抵牾的欲望的牺牲品，多么荒唐！一个人安居一处，他便渴望流浪；等他流浪了，他又渴望有个家。然而知足又是何等兽性的东西——人生一切伟大的事业，都是不知足的人做成的。

> 好人生有三样配料：学习，谋生，渴慕。人应当一路走一路学习；应当为自己和旁人挣得面包；也应当渴慕：渴慕去知晓那不可知晓的。

> 乔治·赫伯特（George Herbert）那首《滑轮》是多好的老诗！伊丽莎白时代那班家伙是真会写！他们也许败在那种想法上——以为诗必须“俏皮”。（记得培根怎么说的吗？读诗使人俏皮。这一句就泄露了他那个时代文学的天机。）他们那些奇思妙喻的双关，如今有点不合我们的时尚了。可是老天！“根本的事”就在他们身上！他们对待人生种种难题，何等豪迈，何等虔敬！

===Original===
> When God at first made man (says George Herbert) He had a “glass of blessings standing by.” So He pours on man all the blessings in His reservoir: strength, beauty, wisdom, honour, pleasure﻿—and then He refrains from giving him the last of them, which is rest, i.e., contentment. God sees that if man is contented he will never win his way to Him. Let man be restless, so that

> Some day I shall write a novel on that theme, and call it *The Pulley*. In this tragic, restless world there must be some place where at last we can lay our heads and be at rest. Some people call it death. Some call it God.

> My ideal of a man is not the Omar who wants to shatter into bits this sorry scheme of things, and then remould it nearer to the heart’s desire. Old Omar was a coward, with his silk pajamas and his glass of wine. The real man is George Herbert’s “seasoned timber”﻿—the fellow who does handily and well whatever comes to him. Even if it’s only shovelling coal into a furnace he can balance the shovel neatly, swing the coal square on the fire and not spill it on the floor. If it’s only splitting kindling or running a trolley car he can make a good, artistic job of it. If it’s only writing a book or peeling potatoes he can put into it the best he has. Even if he’s only a bald-headed old fool over forty selling books on a country road, he can make an ideal of it. Good old Parnassus! It’s a great game﻿ ﻿… I think I’ll have to give her up soon, though: I must get that book of mine written. But Parnassus has been a true glass of blessings to me.

===Chinese===
> 上帝当初造人的时候（乔治·赫伯特说），手边放着“满满一杯福祉”。于是他把库里的福祉尽数倾在人身上：力量、美貌、智慧、尊荣、欢愉——唯独忍住不给他最后一样，那就是安息，也就是知足。上帝明白，人一旦知足，便永远不会回转向他。让人永不安宁吧，好教他——

> 哪一天我要拿这个题目写一部小说，就叫《滑轮》。在这个悲惨而不安的世界上，总得有一处地方，让我们终于能安放头颅、得到安息。有人管它叫死。有人管它叫上帝。

> 我理想中的男子汉，不是那位要把这悲惨乾坤砸个粉碎、再照心头所愿重塑的欧玛尔。老欧玛尔是个懦夫，守着他的绸睡衣和他那杯酒。真正的男子汉，是乔治·赫伯特那块“风干的良木”——无论遇上什么，都做得利落、做得漂亮。哪怕是往炉膛里铲煤，他也能把铲子端得四平八稳，煤甩得正中炉心，一星不洒在地面上。哪怕是劈引柴、开电车，他也能干出一手漂亮的艺术活儿。哪怕是写一本书、削一筐土豆，他也能把全身本事搁进去。哪怕他只是个四十开外的秃顶老傻瓜，在乡路上卖卖书，他也能把它做成一门理想。好老帕纳索斯号！这行当了不起……不过我想，快到把她交出去的时候了：我那本书非写不可了。可是帕纳索斯号，于我一直是真正的一杯福祉。

===Original===
There was much more in the notebook; indeed it was half full of jotted paragraphs, memoranda, and scraps of writing﻿—poems I believe some of them were﻿—but I had seen enough. It seemed as if I had stumbled unawares on the pathetic, brave, and lonely heart of the little man. I’m a commonplace creature, I’m afraid, insensible to many of the deeper things in life, but every now and then, like all of us, I come face to face with something that thrills me. I saw how this little, red-bearded pedlar was like a cake of yeast in the big, heavy dough of humanity: how he travelled about trying to fulfil in his own way his ideals of beauty. I felt almost motherly toward him: I wanted to tell him that I understood him. And in a way I felt ashamed of having run away from my own homely tasks, my kitchen and my hen yard and dear old, hot-tempered, absentminded Andrew. I fell into a sober mood. As soon as I was alone, I thought, I would sell Parnassus and hurry back to the farm. That was my job, that was my glass of blessings. What was I doing﻿—a fat, middle-aged woman﻿—trapesing along the roads with a cartload of books I didn’t understand?

I slipped the little notebook back into its hiding place. I would have died rather than let the Professor know I had seen it.

===Chinese===
笔记本里还有许多；的确，半本都是匆匆记下的段落、备忘和零碎文字——我相信其中有几段是诗——可我看的已经够了。我仿佛懵懵懂懂地一头撞见了这个小个子那颗可怜、勇敢而孤寂的心。恐怕我是个平庸的生灵，对人生许多深处的物事麻木不仁；可是隔三差五，像我们所有人一样，我也会迎面撞上一件叫我震颤的东西。我看见这个红胡子小货郎，就像人类这块又大又沉的面团里的一块酵母：看他四处行走，怎样以自己的方式，去践行他关于美的理想。我对他生出一种近乎母性的心肠：我想告诉他，我懂他。从某个角度说，我为自己逃离自家那些朴素的职分感到羞愧——我的厨房、我的鸡院，还有亲爱的老安德鲁，那个火性子、丢三落四的安德鲁。我陷入一种沉静的心绪。一等只剩我一人，我想，我就把帕纳索斯号卖掉，赶紧回农场去。那才是我的本分，那才是我的一杯福祉。我在干什么呢——一个发福的中年女人——拖着一车我自己都看不懂的书，在路上晃荡？

我把小笔记本悄悄塞回它藏身的角落。宁可死了，也不能让教授知道我看过它。

## XI / 十一

===Original===
We were coming into Woodbridge; and I was just wondering whether to wake the Professor when the little window behind me slid back and he stuck his head out.

“Hello!” he said. “I think I must have been asleep!”

“Well, I should hope so,” I said. “You needed it.”

Indeed he looked much better, and I was relieved to see it. I had been really afraid he would be ill after sleeping out all night, but I guess he was tougher than I thought. He joined me on the seat, and we drove into the town. While he went to the station to ask about the trains I had a fine time selling books. I was away from the locality where I was known, and had no shyness in attempting to imitate Mifflin’s methods. I even went him one better by going into a hardware store where I bought a large dinner bell. This I rang lustily until a crowd gathered, then I put up the flaps and displayed my books. As a matter of fact, I sold only one, but I enjoyed myself none the less.

===Chinese===
我们快到伍德布里奇（Woodbridge）了；我正盘算着要不要叫醒教授，身后那扇小窗忽然滑开，他探出头来。

“哈罗！”他说，“看来我方才是睡着了！”

“咳，我巴不得你睡呢，”我说，“你正缺这一觉。”

他看上去果然精神多了，我看着也放了心。他露宿了一整夜，我原本真怕他要病倒，不过我猜他比我想的结实。他爬上车座挨着我，我们一路赶进镇里。他去车站打听火车的时候，我卖书卖得好不快活。这里没人认得我，我放开胆子学米夫林的那套路数，毫无羞怯。我甚至青出于蓝——走进一家五金铺，买了一只大开饭铃。我铆足了劲摇它，直摇到聚起一群人，然后支起挡板，把书亮出来。说实在的，我统共只卖出一本，可我照样乐了个够。

===Original===
By and by Mifflin reappeared. I think he had been to a barber: at any rate he looked very spry: he had bought a clean collar and a flowing tie of a bright electric blue which really suited him rather well.

“Well,” he said, “the Sage is going to get back at me for that punch on the nose! I’ve been to the bank to cash your check. They telephoned over to Redfield, and apparently your brother has stopped payment on it. It’s rather awkward: they seem to think I’m a crook.”

I was furious. What right had Andrew to do that?

“The brute!” I said. “What on earth shall I do?”

“I suggest that you telephone to the Redfield Bank,” he said, “and countermand your brother’s instructions—that is, unless you think you’ve made a mistake? I don’t want to take advantage of you.”

“Nonsense!” I said. “I’m not going to let Andrew spoil my holiday. That’s always his way: if he gets an idea into his head he’s like a mule. I’ll telephone to Redfield, and then we’ll go to see the bank here.”

===Chinese===
过了一阵，米夫林回来了。我猜他去理了发：无论如何，他看上去干净利落：还买了副干净领子，一条鲜亮的电蓝色飘逸领带，衬他还真挺合适。

“得，”他说，“你那位圣哲要为鼻子上的那记老拳跟我算账了！我去银行兑你的支票来着。他们往雷德菲尔德挂了电话，看样子你哥哥把支票止付了。这事有点尴尬：他们怕是把我当成骗子了。”

我火冒三丈。安德鲁有什么权利这么干？

“可恶的家伙！”我说，“这可怎么是好？”

“依我说，你给雷德菲尔德银行挂个电话，”他说，“撤回你哥哥的吩咐——当然喽，除非你觉得自己做错了事？我可不想占你便宜。”

“胡说！”我说，“我绝不会让安德鲁毁了我们的假日。他向来这德性：脑子里一旦钻进一个念头，跟头驴似的犟。我这就给雷德菲尔德打电话，然后咱们一块儿去这边的银行。”

===Original===
We put Parnassus up at the hotel, and I went to the telephone. I was thoroughly angry at Andrew, and tried to get him on the wire first. But Sabine Farm didn’t answer. Then I telephoned to the bank in Redfield, and got Mr. Shirley. He’s the cashier, and I know him well. I guess he recognized my voice, for he made no objection when I told him what I wanted.

“Now you telephone to the bank in Woodbridge,” I said, “and tell them to let Mr. Mifflin have the money. I’ll go there with him to identify him. Will that be all right?”

“Perfectly,” he said. The deceitful little snail! If I had only known what he was concocting!

Mifflin said there was a train at three o’clock which he could take. We stopped at a little lunch room for a bite to eat, then he went again to the bank, and I with him. We asked the cashier whether they had had a message from Redfield.

===Chinese===
我们把帕纳索斯号安置在旅馆的棚厩里，我去打电话。我心里把安德鲁气了个透，先想接通他本人。可萨宾农场那头没人应。于是我又给雷德菲尔德的银行挂电话，接电话的是谢利（Shirley）先生。他是银行的出纳主任，我跟他很熟。我猜他听出了我的声音，因为我说明来意时，他一点没刁难。

“那您这就给伍德布里奇的银行挂个电话，”我说，“叫他们把钱付给米夫林先生。我陪他同去，给他作保。这样行吗？”

“完全可行。”他说。这只口是心非的小蜗牛！我要是早知道他肚子里憋的什么主意就好了！

米夫林说三点有一班火车他能赶上。我们在一家小饭摊停下来吃了口东西，随后他又去银行，我也跟着。我们问出纳，雷德菲尔德那边可来了话。

===Original===
“Yes,” he said. “We’ve just heard.” And he looked at me rather queerly.

“Are you Miss McGill?” he said.

“I am,” I said.

“Will you just step this way a moment?” he asked politely.

He led me into a little sitting-room and asked me to sit down. I supposed that he was going to get some paper for me to sign, so I waited quite patiently for several minutes. I had left the Professor at the cashier’s window, where they would give him his money.

===Chinese===
“来了，”他说，“刚刚接到。”他看我的眼神却有些古怪。

“您是麦吉尔小姐？”他问。

“正是，”我说。

“能否请您这边来一下？”他客客气气地问。

他领我进了一间小起居室，请我坐下。我估摸他是去取什么单据给我签，便耐着性子等了好几分钟。我把教授留在了出纳窗口，人家就要把钱付给他了。

===Original===
I waited some time, and finally I got tired of looking at the Life Insurance calendars. Then I happened to glance out of the window. Surely that was the Professor, just disappearing round the corner with another man?

I returned to the cashier’s desk.

“What’s the matter?” I said. “Your mahogany furniture is charming, but I’m tired of it. Do I have to sit here any longer? And where’s Mr. Mifflin? Did he get his money?”

The cashier was a horrid little creature with side whiskers.

“I’m sorry you had to wait, Madam,” he said. “The transaction is just concluded. We gave Mr. Mifflin what was due him. There is no need for you to stay longer.”

I thought this was very extraordinary. Surely the Professor would not leave without saying goodbye? However, I noticed that the clock said three minutes to three, so I thought that perhaps he had had to run to catch his train. He was such a strange little man, anyway …

===Chinese===
我又等了一阵，最后连看人寿保险挂历都看腻了。正巧往窗外一瞥——那不是教授吗，正跟另一个男人一起拐过街角，转眼不见了？

我回到出纳的柜台前。

“这是唱的哪一出？”我说，“你们的红木家具固然精美，可我看腻了。我还非坐在这儿不可吗？米夫林先生人呢？他拿到钱了没有？”

出纳是个面目可憎的小个子，留着连鬓胡子。

“让您久等了，夫人，”他说，“这笔交易刚刚办结。米夫林先生应得的款子，我们已如数付清。您不必再奉陪了。”

我觉得这事蹊跷得很。教授总不至于连声再见都不说就走吧？不过我瞥见钟上指着差三分三点，心想兴许是他赶三点的火车，不得不一路小跑。反正，他就是这么个古怪的小个子男人……

===Original===
Well, I went back to the hotel, quite a little upset by this sudden parting. At least I was glad the little man had got his money all right. Probably he would write from Brooklyn, but of course I wouldn’t get the letter till I returned to the farm as that was the only address he would have. Perhaps that wouldn’t be so long after all: but I did not feel like going back now, when Andrew had been so horrid.

I drove Parnassus on the ferry, and we crossed the river. I felt lost and disagreeable. Even the fresh movement through the air gave me no pleasure. Bock whined dismally inside the van.

It didn’t take me long to discover that Parnassing all alone had lost some of its charms. I missed the Professor: missed his abrupt, direct way of saying things, and his whimsical wit. And I was annoyed by his skipping off without a word of goodbye. It didn’t seem natural. I partially appeased my irritation by stopping at a farmhouse on the other side of the river and selling a cookbook. Then I started along the road for Bath—about five miles farther on. Peg’s foot didn’t seem to bother her so I thought it would be safe to travel that far before stopping for the night. Counting up the days (with some difficulty: it seemed as though I had been away from home a month), I remembered that this was Saturday night. I thought I would stay in Bath over Sunday and get a good rest. We jogged sedately along the road, and I got out a copy of *Vanity Fair*. I was so absorbed in Becky Sharp that I wouldn’t even interrupt myself to sell books at the houses we passed. I think reading a good book makes one modest. When you see the marvellous insight into human nature which a truly great book shows, it is bound to make you feel small—like looking at the Dipper on a clear night, or seeing the winter sunrise when you go out to collect the morning eggs. And anything that makes you feel small is mighty good for you.

===Chinese===
唉，我回到旅馆，被这突如其来的分别搅得心里挺不是滋味。好歹叫人宽慰的是，小个子男人的钱总算拿到了手。他多半会从布鲁克林来信，不过那封信我自然要等回农场才见得着，因为他知道的地址只有那一个。也许那一天到底也不远了：可眼下我还不想回去——安德鲁这么可恶。

我赶着帕纳索斯号上了渡船，过了河。我心里空落落的，别别扭扭。连清风扑面的赶路都引不起我的兴致。博克在车厢里呜呜地哀叫，好不凄惶。

没用多久我便发现，独自一个赶帕纳索斯号，乐趣已然折了几分。我想念教授：想念他说起话来那种劈头就是一句、直来直去的做派，想念他那些奇里古怪的妙语。而他竟一言不发就溜之大吉，又叫我着恼。这不合常理。我在河对岸一家农户门前停下，卖出一本烹饪书，才算把这口火气消了一半。随后我顺大道往巴斯（Bath）去——再往前约五英里。佩格的脚看样子不碍事了，我想赶这么远再歇脚，保准出不了岔子。掐着指头数日子（颇费了点劲：恍恍惚惚像离家一个月了），我才想起今晚是星期六。我打算在巴斯歇过礼拜天，好好养养精神。我们端端正正地沿路缓行，我取出一册《名利场》（*Vanity Fair*）。我一心扑在蓓基·夏普（Becky Sharp）身上，路过的人家想买书，我连自己都舍不得打断。我想，读一本好书叫人谦卑。当你看到一部真正伟大的著作对人性洞若观火，你必定自觉渺小——好比晴夜里仰望北斗，好比冬日清晨出门去捡晨蛋时看见日出。凡叫你自觉渺小的东西，对你都大有益处。

===Original===
“What do you mean by a great book?” said the Professor—I mean, I imagined him saying it. It seemed to me as if I could see him sitting there, with his corncob pipe in his hand and that quizzical little face of his looking sharply at me. Somehow, talking with the Professor had made me think. He was as good as one of those Scranton correspondence courses, I do believe, and no money to pay for postage.

Well, I said to the Professor—to myself I mean—let’s see: what *is* a good book? I don’t mean books like Henry James’s (he’s Andrew’s great idol. It always seemed to me that he had a kind of rush of words to the head and never stopped to sort them out properly). A good book ought to have something simple about it. And, like Eve, it ought to come from somewhere near the third rib: there ought to be a heart beating in it. A story that’s all forehead doesn’t amount to much. Anyway, it’ll never get over at a Dorcas meeting. That was the trouble with Henry James. Andrew talked so much about him that I took one of his books to read aloud at our sewing circle over at Redfield. Well, after one try we had to fall back on *Pollyanna*.

I haven’t been doing chores and running a farmhouse for fifteen years without getting some ideas about life—and even about books. I wouldn’t set my lit’ry views up against yours, Professor (I was still talking to Mifflin in my mind), no, nor even against Andrew’s—but as I say, I’ve got some ideas of my own. I’ve learned that honest work counts in writing books just as much as it does in washing dishes. I guess Andrew’s books must be some good after all because he surely does mull over them without end. I can forgive his being a shiftless farmer so long as he really does his literary chores up to the hilt. A man can be slack in everything else, if he does one thing as well as he possibly can. And I guess it won’t matter my being an ignoramus in literature so long as I’m rated A-1 in the kitchen. That’s what I used to think as I polished and scoured and scrubbed and dusted and swept and then set about getting dinner. If I ever sat down to read for ten minutes the cat would get into the custard. No woman in the country sits down for fifteen consecutive minutes between sunrise and sunset, unless she has half a dozen servants. And nobody knows anything about literature unless he spends most of his life sitting down. So there you are.

===Chinese===
“你说的一本好书，作何讲？”教授说——我是说，我想象他这么说。我仿佛看见他就坐在那儿，手里攥着玉米芯烟斗，那张爱打趣的小脸锐利地打量着我。不知怎么，同教授聊天引得我动开了脑筋。他抵得上一门斯克兰顿（Scranton）函授课程，这话我信，还不消掏一枚邮票钱。

好，我便答教授——其实是答我自己——且想想：什么才算好书？我可不是指亨利·詹姆斯（Henry James）那一路的书（他是安德鲁的头号偶像。我总疑心他那是脑充血式的词句奔涌，涌完了也从不停下来好好理一理）。一本好书，该有些质朴的东西。而且，它该像夏娃一样，生自第三根肋骨附近：书里该有一颗跳动的心。一味卖弄脑门的故事成不了大气候。反正，它在多卡斯缝纫会（Dorcas meeting，教会妇女的慈善聚会）上是断断行不通的。亨利·詹姆斯的毛病就在这儿。安德鲁把他挂在嘴边挂得太多，我便取了他一本书，到雷德菲尔德我们那个缝纫圈里去朗读。结果试了一回，大家只好退回去读《波莉安娜》（*Pollyanna*）。

家务杂活、农场操持，我干了十五年，对人生——甚至对书——总归有了几分自己的见识。我绝不敢拿我这文盲见解跟您的较量，教授（我脑子里还在同米夫林说话呢），不敢，连安德鲁的也不敢——可我正如刚才说的，我自有几分主意。我已经懂得，写书跟洗盘子一样，都要下老实功夫。我猜安德鲁的书到底还是有几分好，因为他翻来覆去琢磨它们，确乎没完没了。只要他当真把文字上的杂役干得善始善终，他做个没出息的庄稼汉，我也可以原谅。一个人旁的事尽可以稀松，只要有一件事他干得尽善尽美。而只要我在厨房里是头一等，我在文学上当个睁眼瞎，我估摸着也无伤大雅。从前我一边擦呀、刷呀、搓呀、掸呀、扫呀，一边接着置办正餐，一边就是这么想的。我但凡坐下读上十分钟书，猫就要钻进蛋奶沙司里去。乡下的女人，从日出到日落，没有哪个能连坐上一刻钟的，除非她雇着半打仆人。而不肯把大半辈子用来坐着的人，谁也别想懂得文学。道理就在这儿。

===Original===
The cultivation of philosophic reflection was a new experience for me. Peg ambled along contentedly and the dog trailed under Parnassus where I had tied him. I read *Vanity Fair* and thought about all sorts of things. Once I got out to pick some scarlet maple leaves that attracted me. The motors passing annoyed me with their dust and noise, but by and by one of them stopped, looked at my outfit curiously, and then asked to see some books. I put up the flaps for them and we pulled off to one side of the road and had a good talk. They bought two or three books, too.

By the time I neared Bath the hands of my watch pointed to supper. I was still a bit shy of Mifflin’s scheme of stopping overnight at farmhouses, so I thought I’d go right into the town and look for a hotel. The next day was Sunday, so it seemed reasonable to give the horse a good rest and stay in Bath two nights. The Hominy House looked clean and old fashioned, and the name amused me, so in I went. It was a kind of high class boarding house, with mostly old women around. It looked to me almost literary and Elbert Hubbardish compared to the Grand Central in Shelby. The folks there stared at me somewhat suspiciously and I half thought they were going to say they didn’t take pedlars; but when I flashed a new five dollar bill at the desk I got good service. A five dollar bill is a patent of nobility in New England.

My! how I enjoyed that creamed chicken on toast, and buckwheat cakes with syrup! After you get used to cooking all your own grub, a meal off someone else’s stove is the finest kind of treat. After supper I was all prepared to sit out on the porch with my sweater on and give a rocking chair a hot box, but then I remembered that it was up to me to carry on the traditions of Parnassus. I was there to spread the gospel of good books. I got to thinking how the Professor never shirked carrying on his campaign, and I determined that I would be worthy of the cause.

===Chinese===
修养哲思这门功课，于我是桩新鲜体验。佩格怡然地溜着蹄，狗拴在帕纳索斯号底下，一路小跑跟着。我读着《名利场》，想着千头万绪。有一回我还下车去，捡了几片红艳艳、实在惹眼的枫叶。过路的汽车扬尘又轰鸣，搅得我心烦，可后来有一辆停了下来，好奇地打量我这一车行头，接着便要求看看书。我替他们支起挡板，我们把车挪到路旁，痛痛快快聊了一阵。他们也买走了两三本。

待我临近巴斯，表的指针已指向晚饭时分。投宿农家这一套米夫林的方略，我还有点抹不开面，便想干脆进镇找家旅馆。转天是礼拜天，让马儿好好歇上一歇、在巴斯连住两晚，似乎也在情在理。霍米尼旅馆（Hominy House）看上去又洁净又老派，这名字还逗乐了我，我便进去了。那是家挺上等的公寓旅馆，四下里多是老太太。比起谢尔比（Shelby）的大中央旅馆，它在我眼里简直算得风雅，有几分埃尔伯特·哈伯德（Elbert Hubbard）的做派。那里的人打量我时带着几分疑忌，我险些以为他们要说小店不收小贩；可我在账台上一亮出一张崭新的五元钞，立刻受到了殷勤接待。在新英格兰，一张五元钞就是一纸贵族证书。

天哪！奶油鸡肉吐司，糖浆荞麦饼，我吃得多么受用！自己开火做饭惯了之后，别人灶上出来的一餐，便是头等的款待。晚饭后我一心打算穿上毛衣坐到门廊上去，把一把摇椅摇得轴瓦冒烟，可转念想起，传承帕纳索斯号的衣钵，责无旁贷。我到这儿来，是为了传布好书的福音。我想起教授推进他的征战从不偷懒，便打定主意，决不给这桩事业丢脸。

===Original===
When I think back about the experience, it seems pretty crazy, but at the time I was filled with a kind of evangelistic zeal. I thought if I was going to try to sell books I might as well have some fun out of it. Most of the old ladies were squatting about in the parlour, knitting or reading or playing cards. Mrs. Hominy, the manager of the place, was sitting at her desk behind a brass railing, going over accounts with a quill pen. I thought that the house probably hadn’t had a shock since Walt Whitman wrote *Leaves of Grass*. In a kind of do or die spirit I determined to give them a rouse.

In the dining room I had noticed a huge dinner bell that stood behind the door. I stepped in there, and got it. Standing in the big hall I began ringing it as hard as I could shake my arm.

You might have thought it was a fire alarm. Mrs. Hominy dropped her pen in horror. The colonial dames in the parlour came to life and ran into the hall like cockroaches. In a minute I had gathered quite a respectable audience. It was up to me to do the spellbinding.

===Chinese===
如今回想这段经历，实在荒唐得很，可当时我满腔都是一种布道的热忱。我想，既然要试着卖书，不如从中找点乐子。老太太们多半窝在客厅里，织毛线的织毛线，看书的看书，打牌的打牌。吸烟室里望得见两个干瘪老头。掌柜的霍米尼太太坐在黄铜栏杆后头的账台边，拿一支鹅毛笔核账。我心想，这幢房子恐怕打沃尔特·惠特曼（Walt Whitman）写《草叶集》（*Leaves of Grass*）那年起，就没受过一回惊动。我抱着一种孤注一掷的劲头，决计给他们来点震动。

饭厅门后立着一只硕大的开饭铃，我方才就留意到了。我踅了进去，把它拿到手。站在大厅正中，我抡圆了胳膊拼命摇起来。

你准以为拉了火警。霍米尼太太吓得笔都掉了。客厅里那些殖民时代风范的老太太们全都活了过来，蟑螂似的涌进大厅。不多时我便召集起一支相当体面的听众。该我施展蛊惑人心的雄辩了。

===Original===
“Friends,” I said (unconsciously imitating the Professor’s tricks of the trade, I guess), “this bell which generally summons you to the groaning board now calls you to a literary repast. With the permission of the management, and with apologies for disturbing your tranquillity, I will deliver a few remarks on the value of good books. I see that several of you are fond of reading, so perhaps the topic will be congenial?”

They gazed at me about as warmly as a round of walnut sundaes.

“Ladies and Gentlemen,” I continued, “of course you remember the story of Abe Lincoln when he said, ‘if you call a leg a tail, how many tails has a dog?’ ‘Five,’ you answer. Wrong; because, as Mr. Lincoln said, calling a leg a tail …”

I still think it was a good beginning. But that was as far as I got. Mrs. Hominy came out of her trance, hastened from the cage, and grabbed my arm. She was quite red with anger.

===Chinese===
“各位朋友，”我说（我猜是无形中照搬了教授的生意经），“这只铃平日召唤诸位赴满桌的盛宴，今晚却请诸位赴一席文字盛筵。承蒙掌柜俯允，且为搅扰诸位清静告罪，本人谨就好书的价值略陈数语。我眼见诸位中有几位雅好读书，想必这个题目不算格格不入？”

他们望着我，那份热络劲儿，直赶上一客核桃圣代。

“女士们，先生们，”我接着说，“诸位想必记得老亚伯·林肯那个故事。他说：‘要是把一条腿叫作尾巴，狗有几条尾巴？’‘五条。’你答道。错了；因为，正如林肯先生所言，把一条腿叫作尾巴……”

至今我仍认为这个开场不坏。可我也就说到这儿了。霍米尼太太从怔忡中醒转，急急冲出栏杆圈，一把攥住我的胳膊。她气得满面通红。

===Original===
“Really!” she said. “Well, really! … I must ask you to continue this in some other place. We do not allow commercial travellers in this house.”

And within fifteen minutes they had hitched up Peg and asked me to move on. Indeed I was so taken aback by my own zeal that I could hardly protest. In a kind of daze I found myself at the Moose Hotel, where they assured me that they catered to mercantile people. I went straight to my room and fell asleep as soon as I reached the straw mattress.

That was my first and only public speech.

===Chinese===
“岂有此理！”她说，“真是岂有此理！……恕我请上别处接着讲去。本店不接待行商。”

不出一刻钟，他们已套好佩格，请我上路了。说实在的，我被自己的这股热忱惊得发懵，几乎无从抗辩。迷迷糊糊之间，我已身在驼鹿旅馆（Moose Hotel），那里的人向我担保，小店专门招待买卖客。我径直回房，一挨着草垫子便睡着了。

那是我生平头一场、也是独一场公开演说。

## XII / 十二

===Original===
The next day was Sunday, October sixth. I well remember the date.

I woke up as chipper as any Robert W. Chambers heroine. All my doubts and depressions of the evening before had fled, and I was single-heartedly delighted with the world and everything in it. The hotel was a poor place, but it would have taken more than that to mar my composure. I had a bitterly cold bath in a real country tin tub, and then eggs and pancakes for breakfast. At the table was a drummer who sold lightning rods, and several other travelling salesmen. I’m afraid my conversation was consciously modelled along the line of what the Professor would have said if he had been there, but at any rate I got along swimmingly. The travelling men, after a moment or two of embarrassed diffidence, treated me quite as one of themselves and asked me about my “line” with interest. I described what I was doing and they all said they envied me my freedom to come and go independently of trains. We talked cheerfully for a long time, and almost without intending to, I started preaching about books. In the end they insisted on my showing them Parnassus. We all went out to the stable, where the van was quartered, and they browsed over the shelves. Before I knew it I had sold five dollars’ worth, although I had decided not to do any business at all on Sunday. But I couldn’t refuse to sell them the stuff as they all seemed so keen on getting something really good to read. One man kept on talking about Harold Bell Wright, but I had to admit that I hadn’t heard of him. Evidently the Professor hadn’t stocked any of his works. I was tickled to see that after all little Redbeard didn’t know *everything* about literature.

===Chinese===
第二天是礼拜天，十月六号。这个日子我记得真真切切。

我一觉醒来，神清气爽，赛过罗伯特·钱伯斯（Robert W. Chambers）笔下的任何一位女主人公。头天晚上那些疑虑和沮丧一扫而空，我一心一意地爱着这个世界和世间万物。旅馆是寒碜了点，可要搅坏我的心绪，光这点还不够。我在道地的乡下白铁浴盆里洗了个刺骨的冷水澡，早饭是鸡蛋和烙饼。同桌有个卖避雷针的推销员，还有几个别的行商。恐怕我的谈吐是有意照着教授的口径来的——倘若他在场会怎么说，我便怎么说——不过无论如何，我应付得如鱼得水。那几个跑码头的，起初有一两分钟拘谨腼腆，随后便拿我当自家弟兄看待，饶有兴味地问起我的“行当”。我把我在干的事描绘了一番，他们个个都说，羡慕我来去不受火车摆布的自由。我们高高兴兴聊了老半天，我几乎是不知不觉就布起道来，讲起书的好处。末了他们非要参观帕纳索斯号不可。我们一齐去了停放车马的厩棚，他们在书架前浏览起来。不知不觉间，我已经卖出了五块钱的书，尽管我原打定主意礼拜天一律不做买卖。可我实在没法拒售，他们个个那么热切，都想弄点真正的好书读。有个人一直念叨哈罗德·贝尔·赖特（Harold Bell Wright），我只得承认没听说过这位。看来教授的书架上没进过他的大作。瞧见小个子红胡子到底也有不晓得的文学，我心里暗暗痛快。

===Original===
After that I debated whether to go to church or to write letters. Finally I decided in favour of the letters. First I tackled Andrew. I wrote:

> Dear Andrew:
> It seems absurd to think that it’s only three days since I left Sabine Farm. Honestly, more has happened to me in these three days than in three years at home.
> I’m sorry that you and Mr. Mifflin disagreed but I quite understood your feelings. But I’m very angry that you should have tried to stop that check I gave him. It was none of your business, Andrew. I telephoned Mr. Shirley and made him send word to the bank in Woodbridge to give Mifflin the money. Mr. Mifflin did not swindle me into buying Parnassus. I did it of my own free will. If you want to know the truth, it was your fault! I bought it because I was scared *you* would if I didn’t. And I didn’t want to be left all alone on the farm from now till Thanksgiving while you went off on another trip. So I decided to do the thing myself. I thought I’d see how you would like being left all alone to run the house. I thought it’d be pretty nice for me to get things off my mind a while and have an adventure of my own.
> Now, Andrew, here are some directions for you:
> The Professor (Mr. Mifflin, I mean) has gone back to Brooklyn to work on his book. I’m sorry you and he had to mix it up on the high road like a couple of hooligans. He’s a nice little man and you’d like him if you got to know him.
> I’m spending Sunday in Bath: tomorrow I’m going on toward Hastings. I’ve sold five dollars’ worth of books this morning even if it is Sunday.
> Your affte sister
> Helen McGill.
> P.S. Don’t forget to clean the separator after using it, or it’ll get in a fearful state.

After writing to Andrew I thought I would send a message to the Professor. I had already written him a long letter in my mind, but somehow when I began putting it on paper a sort of awkwardness came over me. I didn’t know just how to begin. I thought how much more fun it would be if he were there himself and I could listen to him talk. And then, while I was writing the first few sentences, some of the drummers came back into the room.

===Chinese===
过后我犯嘀咕，是上教堂呢，还是写信。末了我决定写信。先拿安德鲁开刀。我写道：

> 亲爱的安德鲁：
> 想想真叫人难以置信，我离开萨宾农场才不过三天。老实说，这三天里我身上发生的事，比在家三年还多。
> 你和米夫林先生闹得不愉快，我很抱歉，不过你的心情我完全体谅。可你居然想止付我给他的那张支票，我非常生气。那轮不着你管，安德鲁。我给谢利先生挂了电话，让他传话给伍德布里奇的银行，把钱付给米夫林。米夫林先生并没有坑骗我买下帕纳索斯号。我是出于自愿买的。你要是想听实话——都怪你！我买它，是因为我怕要是我不买，你倒会把它买下来。而且从现在到感恩节，你又要出远门，把孤零零一个人撂在农场上，那种日子我不想过。所以我决意自己来干这一场。我也想瞧瞧，家里大小事全撂给一个人，你尝尝是什么滋味。我呢，也想把心事撂开一阵，自己闯荡一场。
> 听着，安德鲁，这儿有几件事吩咐你：
> 教授（我是说米夫林先生）回布鲁克林写他的书去了。很遗憾，你们俩竟在大路上像两个小流氓似的扭打了一场。他是个挺好的小个子男人，处熟了你会喜欢他的。
> 我这个礼拜天在巴斯过：明天接着往黑斯廷斯（Hastings）去。今儿虽是礼拜天，我一上午照样卖出了五块钱的书。
> 爱你的妹妹
> 海伦·麦吉尔
> 又及：用完奶油分离机别忘了清洗，不然它会脏得吓死人。

给安德鲁写完信，我想该给教授也捎句话去。我心里早给他写好了一封长信，可不知怎么，一旦落到纸上，一阵别扭劲儿就涌了上来。我不知道该怎么起头。我想，要是他本人在跟前，我听他说话，那该多有趣。正在我写下头几句的当口，几个推销员又回到了屋里。

===Original===
“Thought you’d like to see a Sunday paper,” said one of them.

I picked up the newspaper with a word of thanks and ran an eye over the headlines. The ugly black letters stood up before me, and my heart gave a great contraction. I felt my fingertips turn cold.

> Ten lives lost, and more than a score injured
> Failure of block signals

===Chinese===
“想着您兴许乐意看看礼拜天的报纸，”其中一个说。

我道了声谢，拿起报纸，目光扫过标题。一个个狰狞的黑字立在我眼前，我的心猛地一缩。我觉得指尖都凉了。

> 十人丧生，伤者逾二十
> 闭塞信号失灵

===Original===
The letters seemed to stand up before me as large as a Malted Milk signboard. With a shuddering apprehension I read the details. Apparently the express that left Providence at four o’clock on Saturday afternoon had crashed into an open siding near Willdon about six o’clock, and collided with a string of freight empties. The baggage car had been demolished and the smoker had turned over and gone down an embankment. There were ten men killed … my head swam. Was that the train the Professor had taken? Let me see. He left Woodbridge on a local train at three. He had said the day before that the express left Port Vigor at five … If he had changed to the express …

In a kind of fascinated horror my eye caught the list of the dead. I ran down the names. Thank God, no, Mifflin was not among them. Then I saw the last entry:

> Unidentified Man, Middle-Aged.

What if that should be the Professor?

And I suddenly felt dizzy, and for the first time in my life I fainted.

===Chinese===
那些字在我眼前立起来，大得赛过麦乳精广告牌。我怀着毛骨悚然的预感读起详情。看样子，礼拜六下午四点从普罗维登斯（Providence）开出的那趟快车，六点钟前后在威尔登（Willdon）附近冲进了一条没闭合的侧线，撞上了一列空载货车。行李车厢被撞得粉碎，吸烟车厢翻了身，滚下了路堤。死了十个人……我的头嗡嗡直转。教授坐的是不是就是这趟车？让我想想。他是三点从伍德布里奇搭慢车走的。前一天他说过，快车五点从波特维戈开出……要是他换上了那趟快车……

我怀着一种吓丢了魂似的恐惧，眼睛死死盯住遇难者名单。我一个一个往下数。感谢上帝，没有，米夫林不在里头。这时我看见了末尾一行：

> 无名男子，中年。

那要是正是教授呢？

我突然一阵眩晕，生平头一遭，晕了过去。

===Original===
Thank goodness, no one else was in the room. The drummers had gone outside again, and no one heard me flop off the chair. I came to in a moment, my heart whirling like a spinning top. At first I did not realize what was wrong. Then my eye fell on the newspaper again. Feverishly I reread the account, and the names of the injured, too, which I had missed before. Nowhere was there a name I knew. But the tragic words “unidentified man” danced before my eyes. Oh! if it were the Professor …

In a wave the truth burst upon me. I loved that little man: I loved him, I loved him. He had brought something new into my life, and his brave, quaint ways had warmed my fat old heart. For the first time, in an intolerable gush of pain, I seemed to know that my life could never again be endurable without him. And now—what was I to do?

How could I learn the truth? Certainly if he *had* been on the train, and had escaped from the wreck unhurt, he would have sent a message to Sabine Farm to let me know. At any rate, that was a possibility. I rushed to the telephone to call up Andrew.

Oh! the agonizing slowness of telephone connections when urgent hurry is needed! My voice shook as I said “Redfield 158 J” to the operator. Throbbing with nervousness I waited to hear the familiar click of the receiver at the other end. I could hear the Redfield switchboard receive the call, and put in the plug to connect with our wire. In imagination I could see the telephone against the wall in the old hallway at Sabine Farm. I could see the soiled patch of plaster where Andrew rests his elbow when he talks into the phone, and the place where he jots numbers down in pencil and I rub them off with bread crumbs. I could see Andrew coming out of the sitting room to answer the bell. And then the operator said carelessly, “Doesn’t answer.” My forehead was wet as I came out of the booth.

===Chinese===
谢天谢地，屋里再没有别人。推销员们又出去了，没人听见我从椅子上栽下去。我片刻便醒了过来，心突突地转，像只旋转的陀螺。起初我没明白出了什么事。随后我的目光又落在那张报纸上。我发起烧似的把那则报道重读一遍，又读了我先前漏看的伤者名单。哪里都没有一个我认得的名字。可“无名男子”这几个惨痛的字眼在我眼前直跳。唉！要是那是教授……

真相像一股浪潮兜头砸下。我爱那个小个子男人：我爱他，我爱他。他给我的生命带来了崭新的东西，他那勇敢而古雅的做派，暖热了我这颗又老又胖的心。平生第一次，在一阵难忍的锥心之痛里，我仿佛明白了：没有他，我的余生再也熬不下去。可如今——我该怎么办？

我怎样才能弄清真相？当然，倘若他当真在那趟车上，又从残骸里安然脱身，他准会捎信到萨宾农场，好让我知道。无论如何，这是可能的。我冲到电话跟前，去叫安德鲁。

唉！越是十万火急，电话接线越是慢得叫人发疯！我对着接线员说出“雷德菲尔德 158 J”，声音直打颤。我心跳如鼓，等着听那头听筒摘下时熟悉的咔嗒一响。我听得见雷德菲尔德的总机接到呼叫，把插头插进我们的线。我想象得出萨宾农场旧门厅墙上那部电话的模样。我想象得出墙上那块蹭脏的灰泥——安德鲁对着话筒说话时总把胳膊肘支在那儿；还有他拿铅笔随手记号码、我用面包屑去擦掉的地方。我想象得出安德鲁从起居室走出来接铃。然后接线员轻描淡写地说：“没人接。”我从电话亭里出来，前额全是汗。

===Original===
I hope I may never have to relive the horrors of the next hour. In spite of my bluff and hearty ways, in times of trouble I am as reticent as a clam. I was determined to hide my agony and anxiety from the well meaning people of the Moose Hotel. I hurried to the railway station to send a telegram to the Professor’s address in Brooklyn, but found the place closed. A boy told me it would not be open until the afternoon. From a drugstore I called “information” in Willdon, and finally got connected with some undertaker to whom the Willdon operator referred me. A horrible, condoling voice (have you ever talked to an undertaker over the telephone?) answered me that no one by the name of Mifflin had been among the dead, but admitted that there was one body still unidentified. He used one ghastly word that made me shudder—unrecognizable. I rang off.

I knew then for the first time the horror of loneliness. I thought of the poor little man’s notebook that I had seen. I thought of his fearless and lovable ways—of his pathetic little tweed cap, of the missing button of his jacket, of the bungling darns on his frayed sleeve. It seemed to me that heaven could mean nothing more than to roll creaking along country roads, in Parnassus, with the Professor beside me on the seat. What if I had known him only—how long was it? He had brought the splendour of an ideal into my humdrum life. And now—had I lost it forever? Andrew and the farm seemed faint and far away. I was a homely old woman, mortally lonely and helpless. In my perplexity I walked to the outskirts of the village and burst into tears.

Finally I got a grip on myself again. I am not ashamed to say that I now admitted frankly what I had been hiding from myself. I was in love—in love with a little, red-bearded bookseller who seemed to me more splendid than Sir Galahad. And I vowed that if he would have me, I would follow him to the other end of nowhere.

I walked back to the hotel. I thought I would make one more try to get Andrew on the telephone. My whole soul quivered when at last I heard the receiver click.

===Chinese===
但愿我这辈子不用再把接下来那个钟头的恐怖重历一遍。别看我为人爽朗大大咧咧，遭了难，我却像蛤蜊一样闭紧嘴巴。我打定主意，不叫驼鹿旅馆那些好心人瞧出我的痛苦和焦灼。我赶去火车站，想给教授在布鲁克林的地址拍封电报，却发现票房关着。一个小伙计告诉我，要到下午才开门。我从一家药铺挂了威尔登的“问询台”，最后总算接通了某位殡葬人，是威尔登接线员替我转的。一个可怕又满口慰唁的声音（你在电话里跟殡葬人说过话吗？）答复我说，死者里没有一个叫米夫林的，但承认确有一具尸首尚未查明身份。他说了一个叫我浑身发冷的字眼——面目全非。我挂断了。

这时我才头一回尝到孤独的可怕。我想起我见过的那本可怜的小个子男人的笔记本。我想起他天不怕地不怕又讨人喜欢的做派——想起他那顶寒酸的小花呢帽，他外套上缺的扣子，他磨破的袖口上那些笨手笨脚的补丁。在我看来，天堂再好，也不过是驾着帕纳索斯号，吱吱嘎嘎碾过乡间土路，教授就坐在我身旁的车座上。我认识他，才不过——多久来着？他却把一种理想的光彩带进了我灰扑扑的日子。而如今——我是不是永远失去它了？安德鲁和农场都显得淡漠而遥远。我是个相貌平平的老女人，孤独无助到了极点。我六神无主地走到村口，眼泪夺眶而出。

末了我终于又把持住了自己。如今我坦白承认了自己一直躲避的东西，这我并不羞愧。我恋爱了——爱上了一个小个子红胡子卖书郎，在我看来，他比加拉哈德爵士（Sir Galahad）还光彩照人。我起誓，只要他要我，哪怕到天涯海角，我也跟他去。

我走回旅馆，想再试一回，看能不能在电话里找到安德鲁。终于听见听筒咔嗒一响时，我整个灵魂都在颤抖。

===Original===
“Hello?” said Andrew’s voice.

“Oh, Andrew,” I said, “this is Helen.”

“Where are you?” (His voice sounded cross.)

“Andrew, is there any—any message from Mr. Mifflin? That wreck yesterday—he might have been on that train—I’ve been so frightened; do you think he was—hurt?”

“Stuff and nonsense,” said Andrew. “If you want to know about Mifflin, he’s in jail in Port Vigor.”

And then I think Andrew must have been surprised. I began to laugh and cry simultaneously, and in my agitation I set down the receiver.

===Chinese===
“哈罗？”是安德鲁的声音。

“哎呀，安德鲁，”我说，“我是海伦。”

“你在哪儿？”（他的嗓门听着气冲冲。）

“安德鲁，米夫林先生那儿——可有什么消息？昨天那场车祸——他没准就在那趟车上——我吓坏了；你想他是不是——受伤了？”

“一派胡言，”安德鲁说，“你要是想打听米夫林，他蹲在波特维戈的监狱里呢。”

接下来，我想安德鲁准是吃了一惊。我又笑又哭，一齐发作，情急之下把听筒撂下了。

## XIII / 十三

===Original===
My first impulse was to hide myself in some obscure corner where I could vent my feelings without fear or favour. I composed my face as well as I could before leaving the phone booth; then I sidled across the lobby and slipped out of the side door. I found my way into the stable, where good old Peg was munching in her stall. The fine, homely smell of horseflesh and long-worn harness leather went right to my heart, and while Bock frisked at my knees I laid my head on Peg’s neck and cried. I think that fat old mare understood me. She was as tubby and prosaic and middle-aged as I—but she loved the Professor.

Suddenly Andrew’s words echoed again in my mind. I had barely heeded them before, in the great joy of my relief, but now their significance came to me. “In jail.” The Professor in jail! That was the meaning of his strange disappearance at Woodbridge. That little brute of a man Shirley must have telephoned from Redfield, and when the Professor came to the Woodbridge bank to cash that check they had arrested him. That was why they had shoved me into that mahogany sitting room. Andrew must be behind this. The besotted old fool! My face burned with anger and humiliation.

I never knew before what it means to be really infuriated. I could feel my brain tingle. The Professor in jail! The gallant, chivalrous little man, penned up with hoboes and sneak thieves suspected of being a crook … as if I couldn’t take care of myself! What did they think he was, anyway? A kidnapper?

===Chinese===
我的头一个冲动，是躲进哪个僻静角落，好痛痛快快发泄一场，不怕人看见。离开电话亭之前，我尽量把脸色摆平；随后我侧着身子穿过门厅，从侧门溜了出去。我摸进厩棚，好老佩格正在栏里嚼草料。马匹和久磨的皮挽具那股亲切的家常气味直沁我心，博克在我膝边蹦跳撒欢，我把头伏在佩格的脖子上，哭了一场。我想那匹胖老母马懂得我。她跟我一样，滚圆、乏味、人到中年——可她爱教授。

冷不丁地，安德鲁的话又在我耳边回响。方才在大喜过望里，我几乎没留神它们，此刻才咂摸出分量。“蹲监狱。”教授蹲监狱！他在伍德布里奇那场离奇失踪，谜底原来在这儿。准是谢利那个可恶的小个子从雷德菲尔德挂了电话，教授一到伍德布里奇银行兑那张支票，他们就把他抓了。怪不得他们把我塞进那间红木起居室。这背后必有安德鲁。这个鬼迷心窍的老糊涂！我又气又臊，脸上火烧火燎。

我从前从不知道什么叫真正的怒火中烧。我只觉得脑门嗡嗡发麻。教授蹲监狱！那个豪侠仗义的小个子男人，跟流浪汉和小毛贼关在一处，被当成骗子嫌疑犯……倒好像我不会照顾自己似的！再说，他们究竟把他当什么人了？绑票的？

===Original===
Instantly I decided I would hurry back to Port Vigor without delay. If Andrew had had the Professor locked up, it could only be on the charge of defrauding me. Certainly it couldn’t be for giving him a bloody nose on the road from Shelby. And if I appeared to deny the charge, surely they would have to let Mr. Mifflin go.

I believe I must have been talking to myself in Peg’s stall—at any rate, just at this moment the stableman appeared and looked very bewildered when he saw me, with flushed face and in obvious excitement, talking to the horse. I asked him when was the next train to Port Vigor.

“Well, ma’am,” he said, “they say that all the local trains is held up till the wreck at Willdon’s cleared away. This being Sunday, I don’t think you’ll get anything from here until tomorrow morning.”

===Chinese===
我当即拿定主意，立刻赶回波特维戈，一分钟也不耽搁。安德鲁要是把教授关了起来，罪名也只能是诈骗我。总不会是因为他在谢尔比来的路上把他鼻子打出了血。只要我出面把罪名驳回，他们总得放米夫林先生走。

想必我是在佩格的栏里自言自语了——反正就在这当口，马夫走了进来，瞧见我红着脸、明摆着激动万分地对马说话，一脸的茫然。我问他下一趟去波特维戈的火车几点。

“哎，太太，”他说，“听说威尔登那场车祸不清干净，慢车全都停开。今儿又是礼拜天，我看您明儿早上之前哪儿也去不成。”

===Original===
I reflected. It wasn’t so awfully far back to Port Vigor. A flivver from the local garage could spin me back there in a couple of hours at the most. But somehow it seemed more fitting to go to the Professor’s rescue in his own Parnassus, even if it would take longer to get there. To tell the truth, while I was angry and humiliated at the thought of his being put in jail by Andrew, I couldn’t help, deep down within me, being rather thankful. Suppose he had been in the wreck? The Sage of Redfield had played the part of Providence after all. And if I set out right away with Parnassus, I could get to Port Vigor—well, by Monday morning anyway.

The good people of the Moose Hotel were genuinely surprised at the hurry with which I dispatched my lunch. But I gave them no explanations. Goodness knows, my head was full of other thoughts and the applesauce might have been asbestos. You know, a woman only falls in love once in her life, and if it waits until she’s darn near forty—well, it *takes*! You see I hadn’t even been vaccinated against it by girlish flirtations. I began to be a governess when I was just a kid, and a governess doesn’t get many chances to be skittish. So now when it came, it hit me hard. That’s when a woman finds herself—when she’s in love. I don’t care if she *is* old or fat or homely or prosy. She feels that little flutter under her ribs and she drops from the tree like a ripe plum. I didn’t care if Roger Mifflin and I were as odd a couple as old Dr. Johnson and his wife, I only knew one thing: that when I saw that little red devil again I was going to be all his—if he’d have me. That’s why the old Moose Hotel in Bath is always sacred to me. That’s where I learned that life still held something fresh for me—something better than baking champlain biscuits for Andrew.

---

===Chinese===
我盘算了一下。回波特维戈的路其实不算太远。从本地车行雇辆小福特，顶多两个钟头就能把我捎回去。可不知怎么，驾着教授自家的帕纳索斯号去救教授，似乎更相宜，哪怕路上多花些工夫。说老实话，一想到他被安德鲁送进了监狱，我又气又臊，可心底深处，我不由得暗暗庆幸。万一他上了那趟车呢？雷德菲尔德的圣哲到底代行了一回天意。我这就驾帕纳索斯号动身，到波特维戈——嗯，礼拜一早上总归赶得到了。

驼鹿旅馆那些好人，见我风卷残云地打发午饭，当真吃了一惊。可我一个字也没解释。天知道，我满脑子别的心思，那苹果酱就是石棉做的，我也尝不出味儿来。您知道，女人一辈子只恋爱一回，要是它拖到人快四十岁才来——哼，那可就一发不可收拾了！您瞧，我连少女时代打情骂俏这道防疫针都没接种过。我还是个小丫头就当了家庭女教师，而家庭女教师没什么机会轻佻调笑。所以这一回它来时，把我砸得结结实实。女人就是在恋爱里才认清自己。我不管她是不是又老又胖、相貌平平、乏味无趣。她觉出肋下那一阵轻颤，便像熟透的李子从树上落下。我不在乎罗杰·米夫林和我这一对，是不是赛过老约翰逊博士（Dr. Johnson）两口子那么古怪；我只知道一件事：再见到那个红胡子小魔鬼，我就整个儿是他的人了——只要他要我。所以巴斯那家老驼鹿旅馆，在我心里永远神圣。就是在那儿，我明白了生命于我还存着新鲜的东西——比给安德鲁烤尚普兰饼干更好的东西。

---

===Original===
That Sunday was one of those mellow, golden days that we New Englanders get in October. The year really begins in March, as every farmer knows, and by the end of September or the beginning of October the season has come to its perfect, ripened climax. There are a few days when the world seems to hang still in a dreaming, sweet hush, at the very fullness of the fruit before the decline sets in. I have no words (like Andrew) to describe it, but every autumn for years I have noticed it. I remember that sometimes at the farm I used to lean over the wood pile for a moment just before supper to watch those purple October sunsets. I would hear the sharp ting of Andrew’s little typewriter bell as he was working in his study. And then I would try to swallow down within me the beauty and wistfulness of it all, and run back to mash the potatoes.

Peg drew Parnassus along the backward road with a merry little rumble. I think she knew we were going back to the Professor. Bock careered mightily along the wayside. And I had much time for thinking. On the whole, I was glad; for I had much to ponder. An adventure that had started as a mere lark or whim had now become for me the very gist of life itself. I was fanciful, I guess, and as romantic as a young hen, but by the bones of George Eliot, I’m sorry for the woman that never has a chance to be fanciful. Mifflin was in jail; aye, but he might have been dead and—unrecognizable! My heart refused to be altogether sad. I was on my way to deliver him from durance vile. There seemed a kinship between the season and myself, I mused, seeing the goldenrod turning bronze and droopy along the way. Here was I, in the full fruition of womanhood, on the verge of my decline into autumn, and lo! by the grace of God, I had found my man, my master. He had touched me with his own fire and courage. I didn’t care what happened to Andrew, or to Sabine Farm, or to anything else in the world. Here were my hearth and my home—Parnassus, or wherever Roger should pitch his tent. I dreamed of crossing the Brooklyn Bridge with him at dusk, watching the skyscrapers etched against a burning sky. I believed in calling things by their true names. Ink is ink, even if the bottle is marked “commercial fluid.” I didn’t try to blink the fact that I was in love. In fact, I gloried in it. As Parnassus rolled along the road, and the scarlet maple leaves eddied gently down in the blue October air, I made up a kind of chant which I called

> O God, I thank Thee who sent this great adventure my way! I am grateful to have come out of the barren land of spinsterhood, seeing the glory of a love greater than myself. I thank Thee for teaching me that mixing, and kneading, and baking are not all that life holds for me. Even if he doesn’t love me, God, I shall always be his.

===Chinese===
那个礼拜天，是新英格兰人十月里偶尔得着的那种温醇的金色日子。正如每个庄稼人都知道的，一年其实从三月开始；到九月底十月初，季节便到了它完美的、熟透的顶点。总有那么几天，世界仿佛悬停在一场梦一般的甜静里，正当果实最满的时候，凋败尚未开始。我没有（像安德鲁那样的）辞藻去描摹它，可多年来，年年秋天我都留意到它。我记得从前在农场，有时晚饭前我会伏在柴堆上站一小会儿，看十月里那些紫红的落日。我会听见安德鲁在小书房里写作时，他那小打字机的铃叮的一声脆响。然后我便把这满目的美与怅惘往肚里一咽，跑回去捣土豆。

佩格拉着帕纳索斯号，沿着往回走的路，一路欢快地辘辘而行。我想她知道我们是回去找教授的。博克在路旁撒开腿欢腾。我也得了大把工夫想心事。总的说来，我是高兴的；因为我有太多要盘算。一场起初只当是玩笑或一时兴起的冒险，如今成了我生命的正题本身。我大概耽于幻想，浪漫得像只小母鸡，可是凭着乔治·艾略特（George Eliot）的骸骨起誓，我倒怜惜那些从没有机会耽于幻想的女人。米夫林蹲在监狱里；是啊，可他没准已经死了，还——面目全非！我的心怎么也不肯全然悲伤。我正在去把他从卑污的囹圄里搭救出来的路上。望着路旁一枝黄花转成青铜色、垂下头去，我冥想，这季节和我之间仿佛有一种亲缘。我呢，正当妇人的盛熟，濒临向秋天沉落，可是瞧啊！托上帝的福，我找到了我的男人，我的主人。他用他自己的火与勇气点着了我。安德鲁怎么样、萨宾农场怎么样、世上别的怎么样，我都不管了。这儿就是我的炉火与家园——帕纳索斯号，或罗杰支帐篷的任何地方。我梦见和他黄昏走过布鲁克林大桥，看摩天楼映着燃烧的天空勾出的剪影。我信奉凡事要叫真名。墨水就是墨水，哪怕瓶子上贴的是“商用墨水”。我不想眨着眼回避我在恋爱这个事实。不瞒您说，我引以为荣。帕纳索斯号在路上辘辘滚动，鲜红的枫叶在十月的蓝天里打着旋儿轻轻飘落，我编了一支小祷歌，管它叫——

> 上帝啊，我感谢你，让这场大冒险落到我的头上！我感谢你，领我走出老姑娘生涯的荒地，得见一场大于我自身的爱的荣光。我感谢你教我明白：调和、揉捏、烘焙，并非生命的全部。哪怕他不爱我，上帝，我也永远是他的。

===Original===
I was crooning some such babble as this to myself when, near Woodbridge, I came upon a big, shiny motor car stranded by the roadside. Several people, evidently intelligent and well-to-do, sat under a tree while their chauffeur fussed with a tire. I was so absorbed in my own thoughts that I think I should have gone by without paying them much heed, but suddenly I remembered the Professor’s creed—to preach the gospel of books in and out of season. Sunday or no Sunday, I thought I could best honour Mifflin by acting on his own principle. I pulled up by the side of the road.

I noticed the people turn to one another in a kind of surprise, and whisper something. There was an elderly man with a lean, hard-worked face; a stout woman, evidently his wife; and two young girls and a man in golfing clothes. Somehow the face of the older man seemed familiar. I wondered whether he were some literary friend of Andrew’s whose photo I had seen.

Bock stood by the wheel with his long, curly tongue running in and out over his teeth. I hesitated a moment, thinking just how to phrase my attack, when the elderly gentleman called out:

===Chinese===
我正这样咕咕哝哝自吟自唱，快到伍德布里奇时，撞见一辆锃亮的大汽车抛锚在路旁。几个人——一望而知是有见识的阔绰人——坐在树下，他们的司机正对着一只轮胎忙活。我心事重重，本想径直过去不多加理会，可我猛地想起教授的信条——无论得时不得时，总要传布书的福音。管它礼拜天不礼拜天，我想，照他自己的原则行事，就是我能给米夫林的最大的敬意。我把车靠到路边。

我注意到那几个人面面相觑，一脸惊讶，交头接耳说了句什么。有位上了年纪的男人，一张清瘦而操劳过度的脸；一位胖太太，显然是他妻子；还有两个年轻姑娘和一个穿高尔夫球装的男人。不知怎么，那位年长男人的脸看着眼熟。我纳闷他是不是安德鲁哪位文人朋友，我见过照片的。

博克站在车轮旁，长长的卷舌头在牙齿间一伸一缩。我踌躇片刻，正盘算这开场白怎么说，那位老先生先开口了：

===Original===
“Where’s the Professor?”

I was beginning to realize that Mifflin was indeed a public character.

“Heavens!” I said. “Do you know him, too?”

“Well, I should think so,” he said. “Didn’t he come to see me last spring about an appropriation for school libraries, and wouldn’t leave till I’d promised to do what he wanted! He stayed the night with us and we talked literature till four o’clock in the morning. Where is he now? Have you taken over Parnassus?”

“Just at present,” I said, “Mr. Mifflin is in the jail at Port Vigor.”

===Chinese===
“教授呢？”

我这才开始明白，米夫林当真是个公众人物。

“天哪！”我说，“您也认得他？”

“可不是，”他说，“去年春天他不是为学校图书馆的拨款来找过我，我不答应他就不肯走！他在我们家过的夜，我们聊文学一直聊到凌晨四点。他这会儿在哪儿？帕纳索斯号归您了？”

“眼下，”我说，“米夫林先生蹲在波特维戈的监狱里。”

===Original===
The ladies gave little cries of astonishment, and the gentleman himself (I had sized him up as a school commissioner or something of that sort) seemed not less surprised.

“In jail!” he said. “What on earth for? Has he sandbagged somebody for reading Nick Carter and Bertha M. Clay? That’s about the only crime he’d be likely to commit.”

“He’s supposed to have cozened me out of four hundred dollars,” I said, “and my brother has had him locked up. But as a matter of fact he wouldn’t swindle a hen out of a new-laid egg. I bought Parnassus of my own free will. I’m on my way to Port Vigor now to get him out. Then I’m going to ask him to marry me—if he will. It’s not leap year, either.”

He looked at me, his thin, lined face working with friendliness. He was a fine looking man—short, gray hair brushed away from a broad, brown forehead. I noticed his rich, dark suit and the spotless collar. This was a man of breeding, evidently.

===Chinese===
太太小姐们低低惊呼起来，那位先生本人（我掂量他不是个教育委员就是那一类角色）看上去也惊得不轻。

“蹲监狱！”他说，“到底为什么？他莫不是用沙袋闷了哪个读尼克·卡特（Nick Carter）和伯莎·M·克莱（Bertha M. Clay）的人？他可能犯的罪，拢共也就这一桩。”

“他们说他骗了我四百块钱，”我说，“我哥哥把他关了起来。可说实在的，他连母鸡一个新下的蛋都骗不走。帕纳索斯号是我自愿买的。我这就在去波特维戈的路上，去捞他出来。然后我打算求他娶我——要是他肯的话。何况今年还不是闰年（按西方旧俗，闰年姑娘家才好自己开口求婚）。”

他望着我，那张清瘦多皱的脸上盛满善意。他是个相貌堂堂的男人——灰色短发，从宽宽的棕色额头上向后梳去。我留意到他讲究的深色西服和一尘不染的领子。一看便知，这是个有教养的人。

===Original===
“Well, Madam,” he said, “any friend of the Professor is a friend of ours.” (His wife and the girls chimed in with assent.) “If you would like a lift in our car to speed you on your errand, I’m sure Bob here would be glad to drive Parnassus into Port Vigor. Our tire will soon be mended.”

The young man assented heartily, but as I said before, I was bent on taking Parnassus back myself. I thought the sight of his own tabernacle would be the best balm for Mifflin’s annoying experience. So I refused the offer, and explained the situation a little more fully.

“Well,” he said, “then let me help in any way I can.” He took a card from his pocketbook and scribbled something on it. “When you get to Port Vigor,” he said, “show this at the jail and I don’t think you’ll have any trouble. I happen to know the people there.”

So after a handshake all round I went on again, much cheered by this friendly little incident. It wasn’t till I was some way along the road that I thought of looking at the card he had given me. Then I realized why the man’s face had been familiar. The card read quite simply:

> **Raleigh Stone Stafford**
> The Executive Mansion,
> Darlington.

It was the governor of the state!

===Chinese===
“好吧，夫人，”他说，“教授的朋友，就是我们的朋友。”（他妻子和两个姑娘齐声应和。）“您要是乐意搭我们的车赶路，我担保这位鲍勃（Bob）一定乐意替您把帕纳索斯号开进波特维戈。我们的轮胎马上就修好。”

年轻人热心地应承下来，可是我说过，我一心要亲自把帕纳索斯号赶回去。我想，让他亲眼看见自家的移动圣殿，是对米夫林这场晦气遭遇最好的抚慰。于是我谢绝了，又把情形说得更周全了些。

“好，”他说，“那让我尽一分力吧。”他从皮夹里取出一张名片，在上面草草写了几行。“您到波特维戈以后，”他说，“在监狱出示这张名片，我想您不会遇到麻烦。那儿的人我碰巧都认得。”

于是握手作别，一一握过，我重新上路，这一场小小的萍水之谊叫我精神大振。走出好一段路，我才想起看看他给我的名片。这才明白那张脸为什么眼熟。名片上印得再简单不过：

> **罗利·斯通·斯塔福德（Raleigh Stone Stafford）**
> 州长官邸，
> 达灵顿（Darlington）。

竟是本州州长！

## XIV / 十四

===Original===
I couldn’t help chuckling, as Parnassus came over the brow of the hill, and I saw the river in the distance once more. How different all this was from my girlhood visions of romance. That has been characteristic of my life all along—it has been full of homely, workaday happenings, and often rather comic in spite of my best resolves to be highbrow and serious. All the same I was something near to tears as I thought of the tragic wreck at Willdon and the grief laden hearts that must be mourning. I wondered whether the Governor was now returning from Willdon after ordering an inquiry.

On his card he had written: “Please release R. Mifflin at once and show this lady all courtesies.” So I didn’t anticipate any particular trouble. This made me all the more anxious to push on, and after crossing the ferry we halted in Woodbridge only long enough for supper. I drove past the bank where I had waited in the anteroom, and would have been glad of a chance to horsewhip that sneaking little cashier. I wondered how they had transported the Professor to Port Vigor, and thought ironically that it was only that Saturday morning when he had suggested taking the hoboes to the same jail. Still I do not doubt that his philosophic spirit had made the best of it all.

Woodbridge was as dead as any country town is on Sunday night. At the little hotel where I had supper there was no topic of conversation except the wreck. But the proprietor, when I paid my bill, happened to notice Parnassus in the yard.

===Chinese===
帕纳索斯号翻过山顶，我又望见了远处的河，不由得暗笑。这一切跟我少女时代憧憬的浪漫多么不同。这向来是我这辈子的本色——满是最家常的、开门七件事式的际遇，任凭我怎么打定主意要高雅严肃，也常常透着滑稽。尽管如此，一想到威尔登那场惨祸，想到那些此刻必在哀恸的伤心人，我还是险些掉泪。我纳闷，州长是不是刚从威尔登布置完调查回来。

他在名片上写道：“请立即释放 R. 米夫林，并给予这位女士一切礼遇。”所以我不虞会有什么特别的麻烦。这更催着我赶路，过了渡船，在伍德布里奇只停了吃顿晚饭的工夫。我驶过那家银行——我先前就在它的小客室里干等——真想寻个机会，拿马鞭抽一顿那个鬼鬼祟祟的小出纳。我不知道他们是怎么把教授押去波特维戈的，只带着讥讽地想，就在那个礼拜六早上，他还提议把那帮流浪汉送去同一座监狱呢。不过我毫不怀疑，他那副哲人心境，早已把这一切消受妥帖了。

礼拜天夜里的伍德布里奇，死气沉沉，跟任何一个乡间小镇一样。我吃晚饭的那家小旅馆里，话题除了车祸别无其他。可掌柜的收账时，碰巧瞥见了院子里的帕纳索斯号。

===Original===
“That’s the bus that pedlar sold you, ain’t it?” he asked with a leer.

“Yes,” I said, shortly.

“Goin’ back to prosecute him, I guess?” he suggested. “Say, that feller’s a devil, believe *me*. When the sheriff tried to put the cuffs on him he gave him a black eye and pretty near broke his jaw. Some scrapper fer a midget!”

My own brave little fighter, I thought, and flushed with pride.

The road back to Port Vigor seemed endless. I was a little nervous, remembering the tramps in Pratt’s quarry, but with Bock sitting beside me on the seat I thought it craven to be alarmed. We rumbled gently through the darkness, between aisles of inky pines where the strip of starlight ran like a ribbon overhead, then on the rolling dunes that overlook the water. There was a moon, too, but I was mortally tired and lonely and longed only to see my little Redbeard. Peg was weary, too, and plodded slowly. It must have been midnight before we saw the red and green lights of the railway signals and I knew that Port Vigor was at hand.

===Chinese===
